<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:suitable-half-space} 
Let $0<T<\infty$ and let the initial data $(n_0,c_0,u_0)$
satisfy \eqref{eq:initial-data-half-space} below.
A quadruple $(n,c,u,p)$ is called a \emph{suitable weak solution}
to \eqref{che}--\eqref{bound} in $\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)$ if the
following properties hold:
\begin{itemize} 
\item[\rm (i)] 
The functions \(n\) and \(c\) are nonnegative almost everywhere in \(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)\). Throughout the paper, we use the convention \(0\log0=0\). Moreover, writing 

\[ \tilde c:=c+1, \] we assume that $ M_c:= \|\tilde c_0\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3)} =\|1+c_0\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3)}<\infty$, where $c_0$ is the given nonnegative initial concentration.
We require, in addition, that
\[
0\leq c(x,t)\leq M_c-1
\quad\text{for almost every }(x,t)\in\mathbb H^3\times(0,T).
\]
In particular, $1\leq\sqrt{\tilde{c}}\leq M_c^{1/2}$ almost everywhere.


We also require the following global bounds. For almost every $0<t<T$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limit-global-scalar-bounds}
\|n(t)\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}\leq\|n_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)},
\qquad
\|c(t)\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}\leq\|c_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}.
\end{equation}
In the following estimates, all spatial norms and integrals are over
$\mathbb H^3$. There is a time-independent data constant
\begin{equation}\label{eq:data-constant}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm in}:=C\bigl(&1+\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty},
\|n_0\|_{L^1},\int_{\mathbb H^3}H(n_0)\,dx,\\
&\|c_0\|_{L^1},\|c_0\|_{L^\infty},
\|\nabla\sqrt{c_0}\|_{L^2},\|u_0\|_{L^2}\bigr)
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
such that the global entropy--energy estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limit-global-energy}
\begin{aligned}
&\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{0<t<T}
\left\{\int_{\mathbb H^3}\bigl(n(t)+H(n(t))\bigr)\,dx
+\|\nabla\sqrt{1+c(t)}\|_{L^2}^2+\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^2\right\}\\
&\quad+\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}
\frac{|\nabla n|^2}{1+n}\,dx\,dt
+\int_0^T\left(\|\nabla^2\sqrt{1+c}\|_{L^2}^2
+\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2\right)\,dt\\
&\quad+\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}
\left(\frac{|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^4}{1+c}
+n|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2\right)\,dx\,dt
\leq C_{\rm in}(1+T).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
holds. Here $H(n)=(1+n)\log(1+n)$ and
$|\nabla n|^2/(1+n)=4|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}|^2$.
In particular,
\[
\begin{aligned}
&n\in L^{5/3}((0,T)\times\mathbb H^3),\\
&u,\ \nabla\sqrt{1+c}\in L^{10/3}((0,T)\times\mathbb H^3),
\qquad
\nabla c\in L^4((0,T)\times\mathbb H^3).
\end{aligned}
\]


\item[\rm (ii)] 
 The quadruple $(n,c,u,p)$ satisfies \eqref{che} in the sense of
distributions in $\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)$, with the boundary conditions
\[
u=0,\qquad \partial_\nu n=\partial_\nu c=0
\quad\text{on }\Gamma\times(0,T).
\]
The condition $u=0$ is understood in the trace sense.
The homogeneous Neumann conditions for $n$ and $c$ are understood
in the following weak sense: for every
$\zeta\in C_c^\infty(\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(0,T))$,
\[
\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}
\bigl[-n\partial_t\zeta-nu\cdot\nabla\zeta
      +(\nabla n-n\nabla c)\cdot\nabla\zeta\bigr]\,dx\,dt=0
\]
and
\[
\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}
\bigl[-c\partial_t\zeta-cu\cdot\nabla\zeta
      +\nabla c\cdot\nabla\zeta+nc\zeta\bigr]\,dx\,dt=0.
\]
The test functions may be nonzero on $\Gamma$.


\item[\rm (iii)]
For every bounded domain $D\subset\mathbb H^3$ whose closure is compact in $\overline{\mathbb H^3}$,
the following local regularity properties hold:
\begin{align*}
&n,\ n\log n \in L^\infty(0,T;L^1(D)),
\qquad
\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}\in L^\infty(0,T;L^2(D)),\\
&u\in L^\infty(0,T;L^2(D)),
\qquad
\nabla\sqrt n,\ \nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde c},\ \nabla u
\in L^2((0,T)\times D).
\end{align*}
In addition, for every compact interval $I\Subset(0,T]$,
\[
p\in L^{5/3}(I\times D).
\]


\item[\rm (iv)] \textbf{(Local boundary energy inequality).} 
\par Let $0<t_1<\tau<T$ and $0\leq\psi\in C_c^\infty\bigl( \overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(t_1,T) \bigr)$ satisfy the boundary compatibility condition \[ \partial_3\psi=0 \qquad\text{on }\Gamma\times(t_1,T). \] 
Then, for almost every \(\tau\in(t_1,T)\), 
\begin{align}
&\int_{\mathbb H^3} \left(n\log n+2|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2+112M_{c}|u(x,\tau)|^2\right)\,\psi(x,\tau)\,dx\nonumber\\
&+\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      \left(2|\nabla\sqrt n|^2+\frac47|\nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2+112M_c|\nabla u|^2\right)\psi\,dx\,dt
 \nonumber\\
&+2\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      n|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2\psi\,dx\,dt
+\frac14\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      (\sqrt{\tilde c})^{-2}|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^4
      \psi\,dx\,dt
 \nonumber\\
\leq&
 \int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      \left(n\log n+2|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2+112M_c|u|^2\right)
      (\partial_t\psi+\Delta\psi+u\cdot\nabla\psi)
      \,dx\,dt
 \nonumber\\
&+\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      n\log n\,\nabla\tilde c\cdot\nabla\psi\,dx\,dt
 +\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      n\nabla\tilde c\cdot\nabla\psi\,dx\,dt
 \nonumber\\
&-\frac47\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      (\sqrt{\tilde c})^{-1}|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
      \nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}\cdot\nabla\psi\,dx\,dt
 -\frac{16}{7}\sum_i\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      (\sqrt{\tilde c})^{-1}
      (\partial_i\sqrt{\tilde c})^3
      \partial_i\psi\,dx\,dt
 \nonumber\\
&+224M_c\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      (p-\bar p(t))u\cdot\nabla\psi\,dx\,dt
 -224M_c\int_0^\tau\!\!\int_{\mathbb H^3}
      n\nabla\phi\cdot u\,\psi\,dx\,dt,
\label{le}
\end{align}
where \(\bar p\in L^1_{\rm loc}(0,T)\) is an arbitrary locally integrable function of time; in applications it will be chosen as a suitable local spatial average of the pressure. 
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:half-space-existence}
Assume that $\phi$ satisfies the standing bounded-gradient assumption
and that the initial data satisfy
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:initial-data-half-space}
\begin{aligned}
&n_0\geq0,\qquad
n_0\in L^1(\mathbb H^3),\qquad
H(n_0)\in L^1(\mathbb H^3),\\
&c_0\geq0,\qquad
c_0\in L^1(\mathbb H^3)\cap L^\infty(\mathbb H^3),\qquad
\nabla\sqrt{c_0}\in L^2(\mathbb H^3),\\
&u_0\in L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Then there exists a global quadruple $(n,c,u,p)$ solving
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:half-space-CNS}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&\partial_t n+u\cdot\nabla n-\Delta n
=-\nabla\cdot(n\nabla c),\\
&\partial_t c+u\cdot\nabla c-\Delta c
=-nc,\\
&\partial_t u+u\cdot\nabla u-\Delta u+\nabla p
=-n\nabla\phi,\\
&\nabla\cdot u=0
\end{aligned}
\right.
\qquad
\text{in }\mathbb H^3\times(0,\infty).
\end{equation}
The boundary conditions are understood in the sense specified in
Definition~\ref{def:suitable-half-space}(ii).

For every $0<T<\infty$ and every bounded domain
$D\subset\mathbb H^3$ whose closure is compact in
$\overline{\mathbb H^3}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:limit-regularity}
\begin{aligned}
&n,\ n\log n\in L^\infty(0,T;L^1(D)),
\qquad
\nabla\sqrt n\in L^2((0,T)\times D),\\
&\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}\in L^\infty(0,T;L^2(D)),
\qquad
\nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde c}\in L^2((0,T)\times D),\\
&u\in L^\infty(0,T;L^2(D))
\cap L^2(0,T;H^1(D)).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every compact interval $I\Subset(0,T]$,
\[
p\in L^{5/3}(I\times D).
\]

The solution attains its initial data in the weak sense specified in
Section~\ref{subsec:compactness-local-energy} and satisfies Definition~\ref{def:suitable-half-space} on every finite
time interval. In particular, the pressure is locally $L^{5/3}$ up to
the flat boundary on every time interval compactly contained in
$(0,\infty)$; no pressure estimate at the genuine initial time $t=0$
is asserted.
The approximation may be selected as specified in
Section~\ref{sec:eventual-regularity}; this selection also has the
eventual regularity asserted in Theorem~\ref{thm:eventual-regularity}
and the large-time behavior in Theorem~\ref{thm:large-time-asymptotics}.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:local-nature-suitable}

Definition~\ref{def:suitable-half-space}(i) includes the global mass
and entropy--energy bounds
\eqref{eq:limit-global-scalar-bounds}--\eqref{eq:limit-global-energy}.
The global density dissipation controls $\nabla\sqrt{1+n}$;
item~(iii) additionally requires the local Fisher-information bound
for $\nabla\sqrt n$.

The energy quantities in items~(i) and~(iii) are controlled on
$(0,T)$, including near the initial time. In contrast, the pressure is
only required to be locally integrable on time intervals compactly
contained in $(0,T)$. In particular, no pressure integrability up to
the genuine initial time $t=0$ is required. This is sufficient for the
local analysis below, since every cylinder under consideration is chosen
so that its time interval is compactly contained in $(0,T)$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:boundary-regular-point}
Let $(n,c,u,p)$ be a suitable weak solution in
$\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)$, and let
\[
z_0=(x_0,t_0)\in\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(0,T).
\]
We call $z_0$ a \emph{regular point} if there exist an admissible
radius $r>0$ at $z_0$ and some $\alpha\in(0,1)$ such that
\[
n,\ u,\ \nabla c
\in C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}
\bigl(\overline{\mathcal Q_r(z_0)}\bigr).
\]
Otherwise, $z_0$ is called a \emph{singular point}.


The singular set on the time interval $(0,T)$ is defined by
\[
\mathcal S(T)
:=
\left\{
z_0\in\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(0,T):
z_0\text{ is a singular point}
\right\}.
\]
For a global suitable weak solution on
$\mathbb H^3\times(0,\infty)$, we write
\[
\mathcal S
:=
\left\{
z_0\in\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(0,\infty):
z_0\text{ is a singular point}
\right\}.
\]
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{main4}
Let $(n,c,u,p)$ be a suitable weak solution to
\eqref{che}--\eqref{bound} in $\mathbb H^3\times(0,\infty)$.
There exists $\varepsilon_3=\varepsilon_3(M_c,M_\phi)>0$ such that
every point
\[
z_0=(x_0,t_0)\in\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(0,\infty)
\]
is regular provided
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-dissipation-criterion}
\limsup_{r\downarrow0}\frac1r\int_{\mathcal Q_r(z_0)}
\left(|\nabla\sqrt n|^2+|\nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
      +|\nabla u|^2\right)\,dx\,dt\leq\varepsilon_3.
\end{equation}
Here the radii are admissible at $z_0$, so $\mathcal Q_r(z_0)$
is an interior cylinder or a boundary half-cylinder as appropriate.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- maincorollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:full-singular-set}
Let $(n,c,u,p)$ be a global suitable weak solution to
\eqref{che}--\eqref{bound} in $\mathbb H^3\times(0,\infty)$,
and let $\mathcal S\subset\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(0,\infty)$
be its singular set. Then
\[
\mathcal P^1(\mathcal S)=0.
\]
<!-- end maincorollary-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:interior-comparison}

The earlier interior theory includes the vanishing
$5/3$-dimensional Hausdorff measure result at the first blow-up
time in~\cite{CLW2025}, and the subsequent space-time estimate
$\mathcal P^{1+\delta}(\mathcal S)=0$ for every sufficiently small
$\delta>0$ in~\cite{CLWW2023Hausdorff}. The latter already implies
$\dim_{\mathcal P}\mathcal S\leq1$.

For the constant-sensitivity, linear-consumption system~\eqref{che}
and the suitable weak solutions of
Definition~\ref{def:suitable-half-space},
Corollary~\ref{cor:full-singular-set} gives the endpoint conclusion
$\mathcal P^1(\mathcal S)=0$ for the full singular set, including
its boundary part. Theorem~\ref{main4} uses the natural factor
$r^{-1}$ for the density dissipation, in place of the
$r^{-1-\delta}$ factor in the earlier criterion.

The improvement is the endpoint measure conclusion and the
inclusion of boundary points, rather than a dimension bound
strictly below one. The comparison is made within the respective
solution frameworks: the entropy variables ($\sqrt{1+c}$ here
and $\sqrt c$ in~\cite{CLWW2023Hausdorff}) and pressure
integrability requirements differ, so no inclusion of the two
classes of suitable weak solutions is asserted.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:boundary-concentration}
The experiments of Dombrowski et al.~\cite{DCCGK2004} show
concentration of bacteria near the edge of sessile drops,
while Tuval et al.~\cite{ILCJR 2005} describe accumulation near
solid--air--water contact lines. Including physical boundary
points in the regularity theory is therefore qualitatively
consistent with the observed importance of boundary regions.
The result bounds possible loss of regularity; it does not
identify the experimentally observed concentration regions
with mathematical singularities or assert that singularities occur.

The principal difficulties are the nonhomogeneous scaling and
negative part of $n\log n$, the interaction of Dirichlet and
Neumann conditions in boundary decay estimates, and the
coupling of pressure to $n\nabla\phi$. Our main innovations are
a local energy estimate without a separate entropy quantity,
parity-compatible caloric decompositions, and a boundary-compatible
existence and pressure framework. Their roles are detailed in
Section~\ref{subsec:key-ingredients}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- mainproposition-1: body_md -->
\label{main1}
Let $0<T<\infty$, and let $(n,c,u,p)$ be a normalized local
restriction of a suitable weak solution to \eqref{che}--\eqref{bound}
on the unit cylinder $\mathcal Q_1(z_0)$ described above.
For any $\tilde q\in(1,\infty)$, assume further that

$$
p\in L_t^{\tilde q}L_x^1(\mathcal Q_1(z_0)).
$$
There exists
\[
\varepsilon_0
=
\varepsilon_0(\tilde q,M_c,M_\phi)>0
\]
such that, if
\begin{align}
&\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t\in(t_0-1,t_0)}
\int_{\mathcal B_1(x_0)}
\left(
n+|u|^2+|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
\right)(x,t)\,dx
\nonumber\\
&\quad+
\int_{\mathcal Q_1(z_0)}
\left(
|\nabla\sqrt n|^2
+|\nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
+|\nabla u|^2
\right)\,dx\,dt
\nonumber\\
&\quad+
\int_{t_0-1}^{t_0}
\left\|
p-[p]_{x_0,1}(t)
\right\|_{L_x^1(\mathcal B_1(x_0))}^{\tilde q}\,dt
\leq\varepsilon_0,
\label{14}
\end{align}
where
$$
[p]_{x_0,r}(t)
:=
\fint_{\mathcal B_r(x_0)}p(x,t)\,dx,
$$
then $(n,\nabla c,u)$ is regular in
$\overline{\mathcal Q_{1/2}(z_0)}$.
<!-- end mainproposition-1 -->
<!-- mainproposition-2: body_md -->
\label{main2}
Let $0<T<\infty$, and let $(n,c,u,p)$ be a normalized local
restriction of a suitable weak solution to \eqref{che}--\eqref{bound}
on the unit cylinder $\mathcal Q_1(z_0)$ described above. For each pair
\[
q>\frac52,
\qquad
\tilde q>1,
\]
and every $M>0$, there exists
\[
\varepsilon_1
=
\varepsilon_1(M,q,\tilde q,M_c,M_\phi)>0
\]
such that, if
\begin{equation}
\|n\|_{L^1_{t,x}(\mathcal Q_1(z_0))}\leq M
\label{eq:main2-mass-bound}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{align}
&\|u\|_{L_{t,x}^q(\mathcal{Q}_1(z_0))}
+\|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}\|_
{L_{t,x}^q(\mathcal{Q}_1(z_0))}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad
+\left\|
p-[p]_{x_0,1}(t)
\right\|_
{L_t^{\tilde q} L_x^1(\mathcal{Q}_1(z_0))}
\leq\varepsilon_1,
\label{eq:main2-smallness}
\end{align}
then $(n,\nabla c,u)$ is regular in
$\overline{\mathcal Q_{1/2}(z_0)}$.
<!-- end mainproposition-2 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:mass-threshold-data}
The bound in~\eqref{eq:main2-mass-bound} is a space--time
$L^1$ bound, not a pointwise bound on $n$. The threshold is
uniform over all suitable weak solutions obeying the same
mass bound and can be chosen nonincreasing in $M$.
For a global solution satisfying
\[
\int_{\mathbb H^3}n(x,t)\,dx
=\|n_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}
\quad\text{for a.e. }t>0,
\]
every physical unit cylinder contained in the positive-time
domain satisfies~\eqref{eq:main2-mass-bound} with
$M=1+\|n_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}$. Hence its threshold may be
chosen as
\[
\varepsilon_1\!\left(
1+\|n_0\|_{L^1},q,\tilde q,
1+\|c_0\|_{L^\infty},\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}
\right).
\]
No dependence on $\|u_0\|_{L^2}$ or on the initial entropy
is needed for this local threshold; the velocity, signal-gradient,
and pressure smallness assumptions are still required.

For a physical cylinder of radius $R$, the natural rescaling gives
\[
\|n_R\|_{L^1(\mathcal Q_1)}
=R^{-3}\int_{\mathcal Q_R}n\,dx\,dt
\leq R^{-1}\|n_0\|_{L^1},
\qquad
\|\nabla\phi_R\|_{L^\infty}
\leq R\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}.
\]
Thus one can use $M=1+R^{-1}\|n_0\|_{L^1}$ after normalization.
The assertion does not provide a mass-independent threshold
uniform over all observation scales. The small-mass criterion
with a threshold independent of $M$ is retained in the proof
of the dissipation criterion.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:eventual-regularity}
In addition to the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:half-space-existence},
assume that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-potential-assumptions}
\phi\in L^\infty(\mathbb H^3),\qquad
\nabla\phi\in C^{0,\beta_0}(\overline{\mathbb H^3})
\quad\text{for some }\beta_0\in(0,1),
\end{equation}
where the H\"older norm is global.
The global suitable weak solution $(n,c,u,p)$ in that theorem can be
chosen so that there exist $T_*>0$ and $\alpha\in(0,\beta_0)$ for which
\[
n,c,u\in C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha/2}_{\rm loc}
\bigl(\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times[T_*,\infty)\bigr)
\]
and
\[
\sup_{t\geq T_*}
\left(\|n(t)\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3)}
+\|c(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb H^3)}
+\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3)}\right)<\infty.
\]
The equations and boundary conditions hold classically on this
time interval, and $\nabla p$ has the corresponding local
$C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}$ regularity up to the flat boundary.
In particular, the full singular set satisfies
\[
\mathcal S\cap
\bigl(\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times[T_*,\infty)\bigr)=\varnothing.
\]
No smallness of the initial data is imposed.
The eventual uniform bounds require only the additional assumption
$\phi\in L^\infty$; H\"older continuity of $\nabla\phi$ is used solely
to obtain the displayed classical regularity and the corresponding
pressure regularity.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:eventual-winkler}
Winkler~\cite{Winkler2017} proves eventual regularity for an
appropriate class of weak solutions in bounded convex domains.
Our weighted density test is inspired by his joint scalar tests;
we do not claim that this scalar device is new. The argument on
$\mathbb H^3$, however, has a different large-time mechanism.
Finite signal mass and the half-space Nash inequality give
$\|c(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-3/2}\|c_0\|_{L^1}$, independently of the
transport. A weighted density estimate then yields
\[
\int_{T_0+R}^\infty\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}^2\leq CR^{-3/2},
\qquad
\int_{T_0+R}^\infty\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}\leq CR^{-1/4}.
\]
These estimates hold for $R\geq1$.
Since $\phi$ is bounded, the decomposition
$-n\nabla\phi=-\nabla(n\phi)+\phi\nabla n$ gives an effective
solenoidal force in $L^1_tL^2_x\cap L^2_tL^2_x$ on the time tail.
This permits a strong restart of the velocity without a Poincar\'e
inequality. Half-space Stokes estimates and the Neumann reflection
argument then give regularity at every interior and boundary point
after one common waiting time. The proof in
Section~\ref{sec:eventual-regularity} also specifies an iterated
approximation limit that retains the global kinetic-energy inequality
and makes the joint scalar tests legitimate. Accordingly, the theorem
asserts eventual regularity for this selection of the global solution;
it does not infer it from the local solution definition or from the
Hausdorff-measure estimate alone.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:large-time-asymptotics}
Let $(n,c,u,p)$ be the solution selected in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:eventual-regularity}. Set
\[
 M=\int_{\mathbb H^3}n_0,\qquad L=\int_{\mathbb H^3}c_0.
\]
All spatial norms below are over $\mathbb H^3$.
There is $T_1>0$ such that, for every $1\leq r\leq\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-asym-scalar-rates}
 \|n(t)\|_{L^r}+\|c(t)\|_{L^r}\leq C_r t^{-\frac32(1-\frac1r)},
 \qquad \|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-2},
 \quad t\geq T_1.
\end{equation}
The velocity satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-asym-velocity}
\begin{aligned}
 &\lim_{t\to\infty}
   \left(\|u(t)\|_{L^2}+t^{1/2}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}\right)=0,\\
 &\lim_{t\to\infty}
   t^{\frac32(\frac12-\frac1q)}\|u(t)\|_{L^q}=0,
   \qquad 2<q\leq\infty.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 \|n(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|c(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}
 \longrightarrow0.
\]

The density has conserved mass $M$, and the remaining signal mass is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-asym-signal-mass}
 L_\infty:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)
 =L-\int_0^\infty\int_{\mathbb H^3}nc.
\end{equation}
If $L>0$, then $0<L_\infty\leq L$; if $L=0$, then $c=0$.
Define the unit-mass Neumann Gaussian on the half-space by
\[
 G_N(x,t)=2(4\pi t)^{-3/2}e^{-|x|^2/(4t)},
 \qquad x\in\mathbb H^3.
\]
For every $1\leq r\leq\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-asym-gaussian}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^{\frac32(1-\frac1r)}
 \left(\|n(t)-MG_N(t)\|_{L^r}
       +\|c(t)-L_\infty G_N(t)\|_{L^r}\right)=0.
\end{equation}
No initial spatial moment assumption is required.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:large-time-interpretation}
The uniform limit of $(n,c,u)$ is the zero equilibrium. When $M>0$,
however, $\|n(t)\|_{L^1}=M$ at every time, so this convergence does not
hold in $L^1$ for the density. Similarly, if $c_0\not\equiv0$, the
signal converges uniformly to zero while its total mass tends to the
strictly positive number $L_\infty$. Formula~\eqref{eq:main-asym-gaussian}
describes both scalars on the diffusive spatial scale $|x|\sim\sqrt t$,
including convergence in the normalized $L^1$ and $L^\infty$ norms.
The positive remaining signal mass is a feature of the present
finite-mass half-space problem; it contrasts with complete signal
consumption in the bounded-domain setting of \cite{Winkler2017}
with nonzero bacterial mass. The velocity conclusion follows from an energy comparison with the
homogeneous Stokes evolution. No algebraic rate for
$\|u(t)\|_{L^2}$ is asserted under the sole assumption
$u_0\in L^2_\sigma$. The theorem concerns the same approximation
selection as Theorem~\ref{thm:eventual-regularity}.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
(See \cite[Theorem 1.1]{MS1995} and \cite[Lemma 4.4]{DW2020})\label{stokes}
   		Let $q_0$, $r_0\in(1,\infty)$ be fixed numbers. Assume that $g\in L_t^{q_0}L_x^{r_0}(\tilde{Q}_k)$. Then there exists a unique function pair $(v,p)$, which satisfies the following equations:
   		\begin{equation}
   			\begin{aligned}
   				\begin{cases}
   					\partial_tv-\Delta v+\nabla p=g \quad &\text{in}~\tilde{Q}_k,\\
   					\nabla\cdot v=0 & \text{in}~ \tilde{Q}_k,\\
   					[p]_{\tilde{B}_k}=0 &\text{for ~a.e.}~t\in[-\rho_{k+2}^2,0],\\
   					v=0 &\text{on}~\partial_p\tilde{Q}_k.
   				\end{cases}
   			\end{aligned}
   		\end{equation}
   		Moreover, $v$ and $p$ satisfy the following estimate:
   		\begin{align}
   			\|v\|_{W^{1,2}_{q_0,r_0}(\tilde{Q}_k)}+\|p\|_{W^{0,1}_{q_0,r_0}(\tilde{Q}_k)}\leq C2^{bk}\|g\|_{L_t^{q_0}L_x^{r_0}(\tilde{Q}_k)},
   		\end{align}
   		where the constants $C$ and $b$ depend only on $q_0$, $r_0$.

The factor $2^{bk}$ follows by tracking the spatial localization
constants, as recorded in \cite[Lemma 4.4]{DW2020}.
The time lengths $\rho_{k+2}^2\in[9/16,1)$ introduce no further
$k$-dependence: use the zero-initial-data estimate on $(-1,0)$,
extend the forcing by zero before $-\rho_{k+2}^2$, and restrict
the resulting solution to $\tilde Q_k$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
(See \cite[Lemma 4.5]{DW2020})\label{Seregin}
   		Let $q_0$, $r_0\in(1,\infty)$ be constants and $g\in L_t^{q_0}L_x^{r_0}(Q_{k+1}^+)$. Assume that the functions $v\in W^{0,1}_{q_0,1}(Q_{k+1}^+)$ and $p\in L_t^{q_0}L_x^1(Q_{k+1}^+)$ satisfy the equations
   		\begin{equation}
   			\begin{aligned}
   				\begin{cases}
   					\partial_tv-\Delta v+\nabla p=g\quad &\text{in}~Q_{k+1}^+,\\
   					\nabla\cdot v=0,&\text{in}~Q_{k+1}^+.
   				\end{cases}
   			\end{aligned}
   		\end{equation}
   		and the boundary condition
   		\begin{align*}
   			v=0\quad on~[-\rho_{k+1}^2,0)\times \{y|y=(y',0),|y'|<\rho_{k+1}\}.
   		\end{align*}
   		Then we have $v\in W^{1,2}_{q_0,r_0}(Q_k^+)$, $p\in W^{0,1}_{q_0,r_0}(Q_k^+)$, and 
   		\begin{align*}
   			&\|v\|_{W^{1,2}_{q_0,r_0}(Q_k^+)}+\|p\|_{W^{0,1}_{q_0,r_0}(Q_k^+)}\\&\quad\leq C2^{bk}\left(\|g\|_{L_t^{q_0}L_x^{r_0}(Q_{k+1}^+)}+\|v\|_{L_t^{q_0}L_x^1(Q_{k+1}^+)}+\|p\|_{L_t^{q_0}L_x^1(Q_{k+1}^+)}\right),
   		\end{align*}
   		where the constants $C$ and $b$ only depend on $q_0$ and $r_0$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
(See \cite[Chapter III, Lemma 2.1]{G1983})\label{it1}
   		Let $f:(0,\rho_0]\to[0,\infty)$ and suppose $f(\rho_0)\leq C_0$. If there exist $\alpha>\beta>0$ and $C_1$, $C_2>0$ such that for any $0<\gamma<\rho\leq \rho_0$, it holds that
   		\begin{align*}
   			f(\gamma)\leq C_1\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)^\alpha f(\rho)+C_2\rho^\beta,
   		\end{align*}
   		then there exist constants $C_3$, $C_4>0$ depending on $C_0$, $C_1$, $C_2$, $\alpha$, and $\beta$ such that 
   		\begin{align*}
   			f(\gamma)\leq C_3\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho_0}\right)^\beta f(\rho_0)+C_4\gamma^\beta
   		\end{align*}
   		for any $\gamma\in (0,\rho_0]$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:heat-potentials}
Let $1<q,r<\infty$, $I_R=(-R^2,0)$, and
$H_t(x)=(4\pi t)^{-3/2}\exp(-|x|^2/(4t))$ for $t>0$.
For $F=(F_1,F_2,F_3)$ and $g$ in
$L^q(I_R;L^r(\mathbb R^3))$, define
\[
\begin{split}
\mathcal T_R F(t)
&=\sum_{j=1}^3\int_{-R^2}^t
       (\partial_j H_{t-a})*F_j(a)\,da,\\
\mathcal S_R g(t)
&=\int_{-R^2}^t H_{t-a}*g(a)\,da.
\end{split}
\]
These are the zero-initial-data distributional solutions with
right-hand sides $\operatorname{div}F$ and $g$, respectively, and
\begin{align}
\|\mathcal T_RF\|_{L^q(I_R;L^r)}
&\leq CR\|F\|_{L^q(I_R;L^r)},
\label{eq:heat-potential-div}\\
\|\mathcal S_Rg\|_{L^q(I_R;L^r)}
+R\|\nabla\mathcal S_Rg\|_{L^q(I_R;L^r)}
&\leq CR^2\|g\|_{L^q(I_R;L^r)}.
\label{eq:heat-potential-scalar}
\end{align}
The constants are independent of $R$. Under reflection in $x_3=0$,
$\mathcal T_RF$ is odd if $F_1,F_2$ are odd and $F_3$ is even;
it is even if these parities are reversed. If $g$ is even, then
$\mathcal S_Rg$ is even. Data given on $B_R$ may be extended by
zero outside $B_R$ in these statements.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The identities for the equations follow first for smooth data from
Duhamel's formula and then by approximation in the indicated mixed
norms. Spatial Young's inequality and
\[
\|H_t\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^3)}=1,
\qquad \|\nabla H_t\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^3)}\leq Ct^{-1/2}
\]
reduce the estimates to one-dimensional Young's inequality in time.
Indeed, the $L^1(0,R^2)$ norms of $1$ and $t^{-1/2}$ are $R^2$
and $2R$, respectively. Reflection commutes with convolution by
$H_t$, whereas a derivative in the normal direction reverses parity.
This proves all the symmetry assertions. No boundary condition is
imposed on the artificial sphere: these are whole-space auxiliary
solutions, subsequently restricted to $Q_R$ or $Q_R^+$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:caloric-parity-decay}
Let $1<q,r<\infty$.
For $0<\gamma\leq R/2$, the following assertions hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\renewcommand{(\roman{enumi})}{(\roman{enumi})}
\item If the odd extension of $v$ is caloric in $Q_R$, then
\begin{equation}
\|v\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_\gamma^+)}
\leq C(\gamma/R)^{2/q+3/r+1}\|v\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R^+)}.
\label{eq:caloric-zero-trace}
\end{equation}
\item If the even extension of $z$ is caloric in $Q_R$, put
\[
\Phi_z(a)=\inf_{b\in\mathbb R^3,\ b_3=0}
             \|\nabla z-b\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_a^+)}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
\Phi_z(\gamma)\leq C(\gamma/R)^{2/q+3/r+1}\Phi_z(R).
\label{eq:caloric-neumann-gradient}
\end{equation}
\item For a caloric function on a full cylinder, or a function on
a half-cylinder whose even extension is caloric, its ordinary
mean oscillation satisfies
\begin{equation}
\|w-[w]_\gamma\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_\gamma)}
\leq C(\gamma/R)^{2/q+3/r+1}\|w-b\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R)}
\label{eq:caloric-ordinary-oscillation}
\end{equation}
for every constant $b$. Here the norms and the space--time means
are taken on the corresponding full or half cylinders.
\end{enumerate}
All assertions hold componentwise and with constants depending only
on $q,r$. They extend to $0<\gamma<R$ after increasing $C$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The interior derivative estimate for a caloric function $W$ is
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla W\|_{L^\infty(Q_{R/2})}
+R\|\partial_tW\|_{L^\infty(Q_{R/2})}
\leq C R^{-1-2/q-3/r}\|W\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R)}.
\label{eq:caloric-interior-derivatives}
\end{equation}
For completeness, at unit scale take a spatial cutoff
$\chi\in C_c^\infty(B_1)$ equal to one on $B_{3/4}$ and a time
cutoff $\zeta$ vanishing near $-1$ and equal to one on $[-9/16,0]$.
Since $W$ is caloric, $V=\chi\zeta W$ has zero initial value and
\[
(\partial_t-\Delta)V
=(\chi\zeta'+\zeta\Delta\chi)W
 -2\operatorname{div}(\zeta W\nabla\chi).
\]
Duhamel's formula and integration by parts place all spatial
derivatives on the heat kernel and the cutoffs. At evaluation
points in $Q_{1/2}$, the term containing $\zeta'$ is separated
in time, while all other source terms are separated in space.
The heat kernel and its required derivatives are therefore
uniformly bounded on these source regions, even as the time lag
tends to zero. This bounds the indicated derivatives by
$C\|W\|_{L^1(Q_1)}$. H\"older's inequality gives the mixed-norm
bound. The argument applies to distributional caloric functions
by local smoothing. Parabolic scaling yields
\eqref{eq:caloric-interior-derivatives}. The estimates, initially
obtained below time zero, also give the one-sided continuous limits
needed at that time.

For (i), apply this estimate to the odd extension $v^*$.
Since $v^*(x',0,t)=0$, integration in $x_3$ gives
$|v(x,t)|\leq C\gamma R^{-1-2/q-3/r}\|v\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R^+)}$
on $Q_\gamma^+$. The mixed norm of a constant there is a constant
multiple of $\gamma^{2/q+3/r}$, proving \eqref{eq:caloric-zero-trace}.

For (ii), let $z^*$ be the even extension and fix $b_3=0$.
Every component of $W=\nabla z^*-b$ is caloric. The first two
components are even and the third is odd, so
\[
\|W\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R)}
=2^{1/r}\|\nabla z-b\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R^+)}.
\]
Moreover, $\partial_3z^*(0,0)=0$. With
$b_\gamma=\nabla z^*(0,0)$, the derivative estimate and
$|t|\leq\gamma^2$ imply
\[
\|\nabla z-b_\gamma\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_\gamma^+)}
\leq C(\gamma/R)^{2/q+3/r+1}\|\nabla z-b\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_R^+)}.
\]
Taking the infimum over the admissible $b$ proves (ii).
For (iii), apply the same argument to $w-b$ and use
\[
\|f-[f]_a\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_a)}\leq2\|f-b\|_{L_t^qL_x^r(Q_a)}.
\]
This last inequality follows from H\"older's inequality for the
space--time average. In the even case every scalar constant
preserves parity. Finally, restriction of norms and the same
mean estimate give the assertions for $R/2<\gamma<R$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:test-function-compatibility}

The condition $\partial_3\psi=0$ on $\Gamma$ in
Definition~\ref{def:suitable-half-space}(iv) is built into the
local energy calculation below. For the scalar Neumann equations,
the integrations by parts that convert
$\nabla(n\log n)\cdot\nabla\psi$ and the corresponding
concentration terms into Laplacian terms would otherwise
produce additional integrals on $\Gamma$. The prescribed
compatibility condition removes these boundary-flux terms.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:stokes-smoothing}
Let \(1<q<\infty\), \(m\in\mathbb N_0\), and \(v\in L^q_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)\). Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:stokes-smoothing-properties}
\nabla\cdot\mathcal J_\varepsilon v=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb H^3,
\qquad
\mathcal J_\varepsilon v=0\quad\text{on }\Gamma,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:stokes-smoothing-estimates}
\|\mathcal J_\varepsilon v\|_{L^q}\leq C_q\|v\|_{L^q},
\qquad
\|\nabla^m\mathcal J_\varepsilon v\|_{L^q}\leq C_{m,q}\varepsilon^{-m/2}\|v\|_{L^q}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, \(\mathcal J_\varepsilon v\to v\) strongly in \(L^q_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)\) as \(\varepsilon\to0\). If \(v\in H^1_0(\mathbb H^3)\cap L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)\), then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:stokes-smoothing-difference}
\|\mathcal J_\varepsilon v-v\|_{L^2}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\|\nabla v\|_{L^2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
For clarity, let \(A_q\) denote the Dirichlet Stokes operator on \(L^q_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)\). The semigroups generated by \(-A_q\) are consistent on the intersections of the spaces \(L^q_\sigma\), so we continue to write \(A\). If \(w(s):=e^{-sA_q}v\), then \((w,\pi)\) is the unique solution of
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:homogeneous-stokes-for-smoother}
\partial_sw-\Delta w+\nabla\pi=0,\qquad \nabla\cdot w=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb H^3,\qquad w|_\Gamma=0,
\end{equation}
with initial value \(v\). Hence \(e^{-sA_q}L^q_\sigma\subset L^q_\sigma\) for every \(s\geq0\), and, for \(s>0\), analyticity gives \(w(s)\in D(A_q^k)\) for every integer \(k\geq1\). Since every element of \(D(A_q)\) has zero Dirichlet trace and is solenoidal, this proves both identities in \eqref{eq:stokes-smoothing-properties}.

The analytic-semigroup estimate reads
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:analytic-stokes-power}
\|A_q^\theta e^{-sA_q}\|_{\mathcal L(L^q_\sigma)}\leq C_{q,\theta}s^{-\theta},\qquad s>0,\quad \theta\geq0.
\end{equation}
For integer \(k\geq1\), the homogeneous half-space Stokes estimate gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:stokes-elliptic-graph}
\|\nabla^{2k}z\|_{L^q}\leq C_{k,q}\|A_q^kz\|_{L^q},\qquad z\in D(A_q^k).
\end{equation}
Interpolation of the homogeneous graph norms yields, for every integer \(m\geq1\),
\[
\|\nabla^m z\|_{L^q}\leq C_{m,q}\|A_q^{m/2}z\|_{L^q},\qquad z\in D(A_q^{m/2}).
\]
Applying this inequality to \(z=e^{-\varepsilon A_q}v\) and then using \eqref{eq:analytic-stokes-power} with \(\theta=m/2\) gives
\[
\|\nabla^m e^{-\varepsilon A_q}v\|_{L^q}\leq C_{m,q}\varepsilon^{-m/2}\|v\|_{L^q},\qquad \varepsilon>0.
\]
The case \(m=0\) follows from boundedness of the analytic semigroup. This proves the full family of estimates in \eqref{eq:stokes-smoothing-estimates}.

Because \(-A_q\) generates a strongly continuous semigroup on \(L^q_\sigma\), we have \(e^{-\varepsilon A_q}v\to v\) strongly in \(L^q_\sigma\) as \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\). It remains to prove the quantitative \(L^2\) difference estimate. The operator \(A=A_2\) is positive and self-adjoint on \(L^2_\sigma\), and its closed quadratic form is
\[
(Az,z)_{L^2}=\|\nabla z\|_{L^2}^2,
\qquad D(A^{1/2})=H^1_0(\mathbb H^3;\mathbb R^3)\cap L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3).
\]
For \(v\in D(A^{1/2})\), the fundamental theorem of calculus and commutation of the spectral powers of \(A\) give
\begin{align*}
(I-e^{-\varepsilon A})v
&=\int_0^\varepsilon Ae^{-sA}v\,ds
=\int_0^\varepsilon A^{1/2}e^{-sA}A^{1/2}v\,ds.
\end{align*}
Using \(\|A^{1/2}e^{-sA}\|_{\mathcal L(L^2,L^2)}\leq Cs^{-1/2}\), we conclude that
\[
\|(I-e^{-\varepsilon A})v\|_{L^2}
\leq C\int_0^\varepsilon s^{-1/2}\,ds\,\|A^{1/2}v\|_{L^2}
\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\|\nabla v\|_{L^2}.
\]
This is \eqref{eq:stokes-smoothing-difference}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:no-reflection-mollifier}
The normal component is the obstruction to the reflection-convolution construction. Even if an even extension of \(u_3\) vanishes at \(x_3=0\), its convolution with a symmetric kernel need not vanish there. The operator \(\mathcal J_\varepsilon\) avoids this problem and gives both statements in \eqref{eq:stokes-smoothing-properties} exactly, not merely in the limit.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:regularized-global}
Let $\nabla\phi\in L^\infty(\mathbb H^3)$, and let
$(n_0^\varepsilon,c_0^\varepsilon,u_0^\varepsilon)$
be the boundary-compatible regularized initial data constructed above.
In particular,
\[
n_0^\varepsilon\in H^2(\mathbb H^3)\cap L^1(\mathbb H^3),
\qquad
c_0^\varepsilon\in H^3(\mathbb H^3)\cap L^1(\mathbb H^3),
\qquad
u_0^\varepsilon\in H^2(\mathbb H^3)\cap L_\sigma^2(\mathbb H^3),
\]
with $n_0^\varepsilon,c_0^\varepsilon\ge0$ and
\[
\partial_\nu n_0^\varepsilon
=\partial_\nu c_0^\varepsilon=0,
\qquad
u_0^\varepsilon=0
\quad\text{on }\Gamma.
\]
Then, for every $\varepsilon,\tau>0$, problem
\eqref{eq:regularized-half-space-system}-- 
\eqref{eq:regularized-boundary-initial},
admits a unique global strong solution
$(n^{\varepsilon,\tau},c^{\varepsilon,\tau},u^{\varepsilon,\tau})$.

For every $0<T<\infty$, suppressing the approximation indices, the solution satisfies
\[
\begin{aligned}
n&\in C([0,T];H^1)\cap L^\infty(0,T;L^\infty)
       \cap L^2(0,T;H^2), & n_t&\in L^2(0,T;L^2),\\
c&\in C([0,T];H_N^2)\cap L^\infty(0,T;W^{1,\infty})
       \cap L^2(0,T;H^3), & c_t&\in L^2(0,T;H^1),\\
u&\in C([0,T];D(A^{7/8}))\cap L^2(0,T;D(A)),
       & u_t&\in L^2(0,T;L_\sigma^2).
\end{aligned}
\]
All spatial spaces are over $\mathbb H^3$. Uniqueness holds in the
fixed-point class \eqref{eq:fixed-point-norm}. The boundary conditions
hold in the trace sense and the initial data are attained in the displayed
continuous norms. The pressure, determined modulo functions of time,
satisfies $\nabla p\in L^2(0,T;L^2)$.
For every $0<T<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-parameter-high-order}
\begin{aligned}
&\sup_{0<t<T}\bigl(\|n\|_{H^1}^2+\|n\|_{L^\infty}^2
+\|c\|_{H^2}^2+\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty}^2+\|u\|_{D(A^{7/8})}^2\bigr)\\
&\quad+\int_0^T\bigl(\|n\|_{H^2}^2+\|c\|_{H^3}^2+\|u\|_{H^2}^2
+\|n_t\|_{L^2}^2+\|c_t\|_{H^1}^2+\|u_t\|_{L^2}^2\bigr)\,dt\\
&\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,T,\phi,n_0^\varepsilon,c_0^\varepsilon,u_0^\varepsilon}
<\infty.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The dependence on the initial data is through
$\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{H^2\cap L^1}$,
$\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{H^3\cap L^1}$, and
$\|u_0^\varepsilon\|_{H^2}$.
Here the constant may depend on $T$; no polynomial growth rate is asserted.
These bounds suffice for continuation and for the entropy and compactness
arguments below.


Moreover, for every $t\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:positivity-mass-maximum}
\begin{aligned}
&n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\ge0,
\qquad
0\le c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)
\le\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty},\\
&\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^1}
=\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1},\\
&\|c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^1}
+\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}
h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(x,s))
c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(x,s)\,dx\,ds
=\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We divide the proof into six steps.

\emph{Step 1: the closed ball and the elementary nonlinear bounds.} Set
\[
R:=2C_0\bigl(\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{H^1}+\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}
+\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{H^2}+\|\nabla c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}
+\|u_0^\varepsilon\|_{D(A^\alpha)}\bigr)+1,
\]
where $C_0\geq1$ is a fixed constant large enough for the semigroup
and embedding bounds below. Let $T\in(0,1)$ and let \(\mathbb B_R\) be the closed radius-\(R\) ball in \(\mathcal X_T\). For \(U=(n^{\varepsilon,\tau},c^{\varepsilon,\tau},u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\in\mathbb B_R\), the embeddings of the fixed-point space give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ball-embeddings}
\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^\infty}+\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{H^1}+\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^\infty}+\| c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{H^2}+\|u^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{D(A^\alpha)}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,R}.
\end{equation}
Define
\[
\begin{aligned}
F_U&:=\widehat a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})n^{\varepsilon,\tau},\\
G_U&:=u^{\varepsilon,\tau}\cdot\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\widehat h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})c^{\varepsilon,\tau},\\
K_U&:=(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\nabla\phi).
\end{aligned}
\]
Using \eqref{eq:regularizing-extensions} and \eqref{eq:ball-embeddings}, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:nonlinear-source-bounds}
\|F_U\|_{L^4}+\|F_U\|_{L^2}+\|\nabla\cdot F_U\|_{L^2}
+\|G_U\|_{L^4}+\|G_U\|_{L^2}+\|\nabla G_U\|_{L^2}
+\|K_U\|_{L^2}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R,\phi}.
\end{equation}
For example,
\begin{align*}
\nabla\cdot F_U
&=\widehat a_\tau'(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\cdot\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\widehat a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\Delta c^{\varepsilon,\tau}+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau},\\
\nabla G_U
&=(\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau})^{\mathsf T}\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}+u^{\varepsilon,\tau}\cdot\nabla^2c^{\varepsilon,\tau}
+\widehat h_\tau'(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})c\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\widehat h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau},
\end{align*}
and every product on the right belongs to \(L^2\) by \eqref{eq:ball-embeddings}. This proves \eqref{eq:nonlinear-source-bounds}.

\emph{Step 2: self-mapping.} The half-space kernel estimates imply
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Phi1-self-map}
\begin{aligned}
\|\Phi_1(U)(t)\|_{L^\infty}
&\leq\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}+C\int_0^t(t-s)^{-7/8}\|F_U(s)\|_{L^4}\,ds
\leq\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/8},\\
\|\Phi_1(U)(t)\|_{L^2}
&\leq\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\|F_U(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds
\leq\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/2},\\
\|\nabla\Phi_1(U)(t)\|_{L^2}
&\leq\|\nabla n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\|\nabla\cdot F_U(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds\\
\qquad&\leq\|\nabla n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In the last line we use \(\Phi_1=S_N(t)n_0^\varepsilon-\int_0^tS_N(t-s)\nabla\cdot F_U(s)\,ds\); this avoids a nonintegrable two-derivative kernel.\\
For $\Phi_2$ we use
\[
\|\nabla S_N(t)g\|_{L^\infty}\leq Ct^{-7/8}\|g\|_{L^4},
\qquad
\|\nabla^2S_N(t)g\|_{L^2}\leq Ct^{-1/2}\|\nabla g\|_{L^2}.
\]
These estimates give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Phi2-self-map}
\begin{aligned}
\|\Phi_2(U)(t)\|_{L^2}
&\leq \|S_N(t)c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+\int_0^t\|S_N(t-s)G_U(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds\\
&\leq \|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+\int_0^t\|G_U(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds
\leq \|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T,\\
\|\nabla\Phi_2(U)(t)\|_{L^\infty}
&\leq \|\nabla S_N(t)c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}
+C\int_0^t(t-s)^{-7/8}\|G_U(s)\|_{L^4}\,ds\\
&\leq \|\nabla c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-7/8}\,ds
\leq \|\nabla c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/8},\\
\|\nabla^2\Phi_2(U)(t)\|_{L^2}
&\leq \|\nabla^2S_N(t)c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}
+C\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\|\nabla G_U(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds\\
&\leq \|\nabla^2c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\,ds
\leq \|\nabla^2c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For the velocity component, analyticity of the Stokes semigroup yields
\[
\|A^\alpha e^{-(t-s)A}\mathbb P_+g\|_{L^2}\leq C(t-s)^{-\alpha}\|g\|_{L^2},
\qquad \alpha=\frac78.
\]
Since the graph norm of \(D(A^\alpha)\) is equivalent to \(\|z\|_{L^2}+\|A^\alpha z\|_{L^2}\), we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Phi3-self-map}
\begin{aligned}
\|\Phi_3(U)(t)\|_{D(A^\alpha)}
&\leq \|e^{-tA}u_0^\varepsilon\|_{D(A^\alpha)}
+C\int_0^t\bigl(1+(t-s)^{-\alpha}\bigr)\|K_U(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds\\
&\leq \|u_0^\varepsilon\|_{D(A^\alpha)}
+C_{\varepsilon,R,\phi}\left(\int_0^t1\,ds+\int_0^t(t-s)^{-7/8}\,ds\right)\\
&\leq \|u_0^\varepsilon\|_{D(A^\alpha)}+C_{\varepsilon,R,\phi}\bigl(T+T^{1/8}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:Phi1-self-map}--\eqref{eq:Phi3-self-map}, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:self-map-summary}
\|\Phi(U)\|_{\mathcal X_T}
\leq \frac{R-1}{2}+C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R,\phi}\bigl(T^{1/8}+T^{1/2}+T\bigr).
\end{equation}
Choose \(T_0\in(0,1]\) so that the second term on the right is at most \((R+1)/2\). Then \(\Phi(\mathbb B_R)\subset\mathbb B_R\).

\emph{Step 3: contraction.} Let \(U_j=(n_j,c_j,u_j)\in\mathbb B_R\), \(j=1,2\). Since \(\widehat a_\tau,\widehat h_\tau\in C^3\) and the functions \(n_j\) are uniformly bounded, the Moser estimates give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:composition-difference}
\begin{aligned}
&\|\widehat a_\tau(n_1)-\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\|_{L^\infty}
+\|\nabla(\widehat a_\tau(n_1)-\widehat a_\tau(n_2))\|_{L^2}\\
&\quad
+\|\widehat h_\tau(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau(n_2)\|_{L^\infty}
+\|\nabla(\widehat h_\tau(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau(n_2))\|_{L^2}
\leq C_{\tau,R}\|n_1-n_2\|_{H^1\cap L^\infty}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For instance,
\[
\widehat a_\tau(n_1)\nabla c_1-\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\nabla c_2
=\bigl(\widehat a_\tau(n_1)-\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\bigr)\nabla c_1
+\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\nabla(c_1-c_2),
\]
and
\[
u_1\cdot\nabla c_1-u_2\cdot\nabla c_2
=(u_1-u_2)\cdot\nabla c_1+u_2\cdot\nabla(c_1-c_2).
\]
To make the Lipschitz estimate explicit, we expand the differences directly.
Then we have
\begin{align*}
F_{U_1}-F_{U_2}
&=\bigl(\widehat a_\tau(n_1)-\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\bigr)\,\nabla c_1+\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\nabla(c_1-c_2)
\\ &\quad+\mathcal J_\varepsilon(u_1-u_2)\,n_1+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u_2)(n_1-n_2),\\
G_{U_1}-G_{U_2}
&=(u_1-u_2)\cdot\nabla c_1+u_2\cdot\nabla(c_1-c_2)
\\ &\quad+\bigl(\widehat h_\tau(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau(n_2)\bigr)\,c_1+\widehat h_\tau(n_2)(c_1-c_2),\\
K_{U_1}-K_{U_2}
&=\mathcal J_\varepsilon(u_1-u_2)\cdot\nabla u_1+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u_2)\cdot\nabla(u_1-u_2)\\ &\quad+\mathcal K_\varepsilon((n_1-n_2)\nabla\phi).
\end{align*}
Moreover, because \(\nabla\cdot (\mathcal J_\varepsilon u_j)=0\),
\begin{align*}
\nabla\cdot(F_{U_1}-F_{U_2})={}&
\bigl(\widehat a_\tau'(n_1)-\widehat a_\tau'(n_2)\bigr)\nabla n_1\cdot\nabla c_1
+\widehat a_\tau'(n_2)\nabla(n_1-n_2)\cdot\nabla c_1\\
&+\widehat a_\tau'(n_2)\nabla n_2\cdot\nabla(c_1-c_2)
\\ &+\bigl(\widehat a_\tau(n_1)-\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\bigr)\,\Delta c_1+\widehat a_\tau(n_2)\Delta(c_1-c_2)\\
&+\mathcal J_\varepsilon(u_1-u_2)\cdot\nabla n_1+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u_2)\cdot\nabla(n_1-n_2).
\end{align*}
The corresponding differentiated identity for the reaction part is
\begin{align*}
\nabla\bigl(\widehat h_\tau(n_1)c_1-\widehat h_\tau(n_2)c_2\bigr)
=&\nabla\bigl(\widehat h_\tau(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau(n_2)\bigr)c_1+\bigl(\widehat h_\tau(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau(n_2)\bigr)\,\nabla c_1\\
&+\widehat h_\tau'(n_2)\nabla n_2(c_1-c_2)
+\widehat h_\tau(n_2)\nabla(c_1-c_2),
\end{align*}
where
\[
\nabla\bigl(\widehat h_\tau(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau(n_2)\bigr)
=\bigl(\widehat h_\tau'(n_1)-\widehat h_\tau'(n_2)\bigr)\nabla n_1
+\widehat h_\tau'(n_2)\nabla(n_1-n_2).
\]
Using these formulas, \eqref{eq:composition-difference}, the embeddings in \eqref{eq:fixed-point-embeddings}, and boundedness of \(\mathcal J_\varepsilon\) and \(\mathcal K_\varepsilon\), each product is estimated in the same space as its counterpart in \eqref{eq:nonlinear-source-bounds}. We obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:source-difference-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
&\|F_{U_1}-F_{U_2}\|_{L^4}+\|F_{U_1}-F_{U_2}\|_{L^2}
+\|\nabla\cdot(F_{U_1}-F_{U_2})\|_{L^2}\\
&\quad
+\|G_{U_1}-G_{U_2}\|_{L^4}+\|G_{U_1}-G_{U_2}\|_{L^2}
+\|\nabla(G_{U_1}-G_{U_2})\|_{L^2}
+\|K_{U_1}-K_{U_2}\|_{L^2}\\
&\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R,\phi}\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Applying the kernel estimates component by component gives
\begin{align*}
&\|\Phi_1(U_1)-\Phi_1(U_2)\|_{L^\infty(0,T;L^\infty)}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/8}\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T},\\
&\|\Phi_1(U_1)-\Phi_1(U_2)\|_{L^\infty(0,T;H^1)}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/2}\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T},\\
&\|\Phi_2(U_1)-\Phi_2(U_2)\|_{L^\infty(0,T;L^2)}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T},\\
&\|\nabla(\Phi_2(U_1)-\Phi_2(U_2))\|_{L^\infty(0,T;L^\infty)}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/8}\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T},\\
&\|\nabla^2(\Phi_2(U_1)-\Phi_2(U_2))\|_{L^\infty(0,T;L^2)}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R}T^{1/2}\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T},\\
&\|\Phi_3(U_1)-\Phi_3(U_2)\|_{L^\infty(0,T;D(A^\alpha))}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,R,\phi}(T^{1/8}+T)\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T}.
\end{align*}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contraction-summary}
\|\Phi(U_1)-\Phi(U_2)\|_{\mathcal X_T}
\leq C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R,\phi}\bigl(T^{1/8}+T^{1/2}+T\bigr)
\|U_1-U_2\|_{\mathcal X_T}.
\end{equation}
We choose \(T_0\in(0,1]\) so that simultaneously
\[
C_{\varepsilon,\tau,R,\phi}\bigl(T_0^{1/8}+T_0^{1/2}+T_0\bigr)
\leq \min\{(R+1)/2,\tfrac12\}.
\]
Then \(\Phi\) maps \(\mathbb B_R\) into itself and has Lipschitz constant at most \(1/2\) there. Banach's fixed-point theorem therefore gives a unique \(U=(n,c,u)\in\mathbb B_R\) satisfying
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fixed-point-identity}
\Phi(n^{\varepsilon,\tau},c^{\varepsilon,\tau},u^{\varepsilon,\tau})=(n^{\varepsilon,\tau},c^{\varepsilon,\tau},u^{\varepsilon,\tau}).
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:fixed-point-components}, this fixed point is the mild solution of the auxiliary system with \(\widehat a_\tau,\widehat h_\tau\). Standard parabolic and Stokes bootstrap, using the smooth initial data and the fixed positive parameters \(\varepsilon,\tau\), makes the solution classical on \((0,T_0]\).

\emph{Step 4: nonnegativity.} Let \(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-:=\max\{-n^{\varepsilon,\tau},0\}\). We multiply the auxiliary cell equation by \(-n_-\). With all boundary terms on the left, the transport, diffusion, and chemotactic integrations produce the flat-boundary contribution
\[
\frac12\int_\Gamma (n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-)^2 (\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nu\,dS
+\int_\Gamma \partial_\nu n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\,n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\,dS
-\int_\Gamma \widehat a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\partial_\nu c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\,n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\,dS.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:stokes-smoothing}, \((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})=0\) on \(\Gamma\), while \eqref{eq:regularized-boundary-initial} gives \(\partial_\nu n^{\varepsilon,\tau}=\partial_\nu c^{\varepsilon,\tau}=0\). Hence all three boundary integrals vanish. Since \(\nabla\cdot (\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})=0\), the interior transport integral is also zero, and we obtain
\[
\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2+\|\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2
=-\int_{\{n<0\}}\widehat a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\cdot\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\,dx.
\]
By \eqref{eq:regularizing-extensions}, \(|\widehat a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})|\leq C_\tau n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\) on \(\{n^{\varepsilon,\tau}<0\}\). Hence
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2+\|\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2
\leq C_\tau\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^\infty}^2\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
Since \(n_0^\varepsilon\geq0\), Gronwall's inequality gives \(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-=0\). Thus \(n\geq0\), and the auxiliary functions coincide with \(a_\tau,h_\tau\).

Next let \(c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-:=\max\{-c^{\varepsilon,\tau},0\}\) and test the concentration equation by \(-c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\). With the same convention, the only flat-boundary contribution is
\[
\frac12\int_\Gamma (c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-)^2u\cdot\nu\,dS
+\int_\Gamma\partial_\nu c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\,c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\,dS=0,
\]
because \(u^{\varepsilon,\tau}=0\) and \(\partial_\nu c^{\varepsilon,\tau}=0\) on \(\Gamma\). The interior transport term is zero because \(\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon,\tau}=0\). Therefore
\[
\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\|c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2+\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-\|_{L^2}^2
+\int_{\mathbb H^3}h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})(c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-)^2\,dx=0.
\]
Since \(c_0^\varepsilon\geq0\), it follows that \(c^{\varepsilon,\tau}_-=0\), and hence \(c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\geq0\).

\par\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 5. Mass conservation for $n$, mass balance
for $c$, and the maximum principle.}



We first justify the propagation of spatial integrability.
Fix a compact time interval $[0,S]$ contained in the interval
of existence, and suppress the approximation indices.
By nonnegativity, the cell equation can be written as
\[
\partial_t n-\Delta n
+\nabla\cdot\left[n\left(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u
+\frac{\nabla c}{1+\tau n}\right)\right]=0.
\]
The fixed-point bounds imply that $\mathcal J_\varepsilon u+\nabla c/(1+\tau n)$ and $u$ are bounded
on $\mathbb H^3\times(0,S)$.

For $R\geq1$, set
\[
w_R(x)=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\sqrt{1+|x|^2}}{R}\right).
\]
Then $\partial_\nu w_R=0$ on $\Gamma$ and
\[
|\nabla w_R|+|\Delta w_R|\leq \frac{C}{R}w_R.
\]
Testing the two scalar equations with $w_R$, first inserting
an additional compact spatial cutoff, gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb H^3}n\,w_R\,dx
&\leq\frac{C_S}{R}\int_{\mathbb H^3}n\,w_R\,dx,\\
\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c\,w_R\,dx
&\leq\frac{C_S}{R}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c\,w_R\,dx.
\end{aligned}
\]
In the concentration estimate, the nonnegative reaction term is discarded.
Gronwall's inequality and monotone convergence as $R\to\infty$
therefore imply
\[
\sup_{0\leq t\leq S}\|n(t)\|_{L^1}
\leq \|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1},
\qquad
\sup_{0\leq t\leq S}\|c(t)\|_{L^1}
\leq \|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}.
\]
The compact-cutoff calculations below are consequently
justified.

\par Let $\chi_R$ be the cutoff introduced above.  Testing the cell
equation by $\chi_R$ and using
\[
\nabla\cdot (\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})=0,
\qquad
(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})=0,
\qquad
\partial_\nu n^{\varepsilon,\tau}
=
\partial_\nu c^{\varepsilon,\tau}=0
\quad\text{on }\Gamma,
\]
we obtain
\begin{align}
\frac{d}{dt}
\int_{\mathbb H^3} n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\chi_R\,dx
={}&
\int_{\mathbb H^3}
n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\Delta\chi_R\,dx
+
\int_{\mathbb H^3}
n^{\varepsilon,\tau}
(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla\chi_R\,dx
\nonumber\\
&+
\int_{\mathbb H^3}
a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})
\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\cdot\nabla\chi_R\,dx.
\label{eq:mass-cutoff-n}
\end{align}
For fixed $\varepsilon,\tau>0$,
\[
n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\in L^1\cap L^2,
\qquad
(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\in L^\infty,
\qquad
a_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\in L^1,
\]
on every finite time interval.  Hence, by the bounds on
$\chi_R$,
the three terms on the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:mass-cutoff-n} converge to zero as $R\to\infty$.
Therefore
\[
\frac{d}{dt}
\int_{\mathbb H^3}n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(x,t)\,dx=0,
\]
and consequently
\begin{equation}
\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}
=
\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1(\mathbb H^3)}.
\label{eq:n-mass-conservation}
\end{equation}

Similarly, testing the concentration equation by $\chi_R$ gives
\begin{align*}
\frac{d}{dt}
\int_{\mathbb H^3}c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\chi_R\,dx
+
\int_{\mathbb H^3}
h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})
c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\chi_R\,dx
={}&
\int_{\mathbb H^3}
c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\Delta\chi_R\,dx\\
&+
\int_{\mathbb H^3}
c^{\varepsilon,\tau}
u^{\varepsilon,\tau}\cdot\nabla\chi_R\,dx.
\end{align*}
Letting $R\to\infty$ yields
\[
\|c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^1}
+
\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}
h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\,dxds
=
\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}.
\]
The $L^\infty$ estimate follows from the maximum principle:
\[
0\leq c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(x,t)
\leq \|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}.
\]


\emph{Step 6: continuation in the fixed-point spaces.}
Fix $\varepsilon,\tau>0$ and suppress these indices. Let $T_*$ be the
maximal existence time. Fix $0<T<\infty$ and
$0<S<\min\{T,T_*\}$. Throughout this step, all time norms are taken
over $(0,S)$, and $K_T$ denotes a positive constant, which may change
from line to line, depending only on
$\varepsilon,\tau,T,\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}$ and the regularized
initial data, but not on $S$. The energy calculations below are
justified by the local classical regularity and the cutoff procedure
described above. The flat-boundary terms vanish by
$u=(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)=0$ and $\partial_\nu n=\partial_\nu c=0$.

Testing the concentration equation by $c$, using
$\nabla\cdot u=0$ and $h_\tau(n)\geq0$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-c-L2}
\sup_{0<t<S}\|c(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+\int_0^S\|\nabla c\|_{L^2}^2\,dt
\leq C\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{equation}
Similarly, testing the cell equation by $n$ and using
$0\leq a_\tau(n)\leq\tau^{-1}$ yields
\[
\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\|n\|_{L^2}^2+\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}^2
=\int_{\mathbb H^3}a_\tau(n)\nabla c\cdot\nabla n\,dx
\leq\frac12\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}^2
+\frac{1}{2\tau^2}\|\nabla c\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:fixed-c-L2}, this implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-n-L2}
\sup_{0<t<S}\|n(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+\int_0^S\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}^2\,dt
\leq C\bigl(\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}^2
+\tau^{-2}\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}^2\bigr).
\end{equation}
For the velocity equation, the $L^{6/5}$-boundedness of
$\mathcal K_\varepsilon$, Sobolev embedding, and interpolation give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|\int_{\mathbb H^3}\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\cdot u\,dx\right|
&\leq C\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}
\|n\|_{L^{6/5}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}\\
&\leq\frac12\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2
+C\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}^2
\|n\|_{L^1}^{4/3}\|n\|_{L^2}^{2/3}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Testing by $u$ and using mass conservation and
\eqref{eq:fixed-n-L2}, we therefore obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-u-L2}
\sup_{0<t<S}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+\int_0^S\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2\,dt
\leq K_T.
\end{equation}
The smoothing properties of $\mathcal J_\varepsilon$ and the
$L^2$-boundedness of $\mathcal K_\varepsilon$ now imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-epsilon-Linfty}
\sup_{0<t<S}\bigl(\|(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
+\|\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)(t)\|_{L^2}\bigr)\leq K_T.
\end{equation}

We use the standard linear regularity estimates for the Neumann
heat equation and the no-slip Stokes system; see
\cite{DHP2003,DHP2001}. For $z_t-\Delta z=F$ with homogeneous
Neumann boundary condition, and $m=0,1$, these estimates read
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Neumann-high-order-estimate}
\begin{aligned}
&\|z\|_{L^\infty H^{m+1}}^2
+\|z\|_{L^2 H^{m+2}}^2+\|z_t\|_{L^2 H^m}^2\\
&\qquad\leq C\bigl(\|z(0)\|_{H^{m+1}}^2
+\|F\|_{L^2 H^m}^2+\|z\|_{L^2L^2}^2\bigr),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where $z(0)\in H_N^2$ is required when $m=1$.
For the Stokes system, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Stokes-high-order-estimate}
\begin{aligned}
&\|u\|_{L^\infty H^1}^2+\|u\|_{L^2H^2}^2
+\|u_t\|_{L^2L^2}^2+\|\nabla p\|_{L^2L^2}^2\\
&\qquad\leq C\bigl(\|u_0^\varepsilon\|_{H^1}^2
+\|\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla u+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\bigr)\|_{L^2L^2}^2+\|u\|_{L^2L^2}^2\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The constants in these estimates can be chosen independently of $S$.

First, \eqref{eq:fixed-u-L2} and
\eqref{eq:fixed-epsilon-Linfty} give
\[
\|\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla u+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\bigr)\|_{L^2L^2}
\leq\|(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|\nabla u\|_{L^2L^2}
+S^{1/2}\|\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\|_{L^\infty L^2}\leq K_T.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:Stokes-high-order-estimate}, we obtain
\[
\|u\|_{L^\infty H^1}+\|u\|_{L^2H^2}
+\|u_t\|_{L^2L^2}+\|\nabla p\|_{L^2L^2}\leq K_T.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\|\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla u+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\bigr)\|_{L^\infty L^2}
\leq\|(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}
\|\nabla u\|_{L^\infty L^2}
+\|\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\|_{L^\infty L^2}\leq K_T.
\]
The Stokes mild formula, the boundedness of the Helmholtz projection
on $L^2$, and $\alpha=7/8<1$ therefore yield
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-u-hierarchy-explicit}
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{0<t<S}\|u(t)\|_{D(A^\alpha)}
&\leq C\|u_0^\varepsilon\|_{D(A^\alpha)}
+K_T\int_0^T(1+s^{-\alpha})\,ds\\
&\leq K_T.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Since $D(A^{7/8})\hookrightarrow H^{7/4}
\hookrightarrow L^\infty\cap W^{1,4}$, it follows that
\[
\|u\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}
+\|\nabla u\|_{L^\infty L^4}\leq K_T.
\]

For the concentration equation,
\eqref{eq:fixed-c-L2}--\eqref{eq:fixed-n-L2} and the preceding
velocity bound give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\bigl(u\cdot\nabla c+h_\tau(n)c\bigr)\|_{L^2L^2}
&\leq\|u\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|\nabla c\|_{L^2L^2}
+S^{1/2}\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}\|n\|_{L^\infty L^2}\\
&\leq K_T.
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:Neumann-high-order-estimate} with $m=0$ to $c$
therefore gives
\[
\|c\|_{L^\infty H^1}+\|c\|_{L^2H^2}
+\|c_t\|_{L^2L^2}\leq K_T.
\]
Next, use the concentration mild formula and
$0\leq h_\tau(n)c\leq\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}n$.
The $L^4$ gradient estimate for the Neumann heat semigroup gives,
for $0<t<S$,
\[
\|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^4}
\leq K_T
+K_T\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\|\nabla c(s)\|_{L^4}\,ds
+K_T\int_0^t(t-s)^{-7/8}\,ds.
\]
Here the transport term is estimated in $L^4$, whereas the reaction
term is estimated in $L^2$ using \eqref{eq:fixed-n-L2}.
The fractional Gronwall inequality gives a uniform $L^4$ bound
for $\nabla c$. Combining this with the preceding $H^1$ bound yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-c-gradient-powers}
\sup_{0<t<S}\bigl(\|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^2}
+\|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^4}\bigr)\leq K_T.
\end{equation}

The cell flux satisfies
\[
\sup_{0<t<S}\|a_\tau(n)\nabla c+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)n\|_{L^2}
\leq\tau^{-1}\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty L^2}
+\|(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|n\|_{L^\infty L^2}
\leq K_T.
\]
Its normal component vanishes on $\Gamma$. Hence the cell mild
formula and the $L^2$ to $L^4$ estimate for $\mathcal T_N$ imply
\[
\|n(t)\|_{L^4}
\leq C\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^4}
+K_T\int_0^t(t-s)^{-7/8}\,ds\leq K_T.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:fixed-c-gradient-powers}, this bounds the
same flux in $L^\infty(0,S;L^4)$. The $L^4$ to $L^\infty$
estimate for $\mathcal T_N$, again with singularity
$(t-s)^{-7/8}$, then gives $\|n\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\leq K_T$.
Moreover,
\[
\sup_{0<t<S}\|u\cdot\nabla c+h_\tau(n)c\|_{L^4}
\leq\|u\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty L^4}
+\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}\|n\|_{L^\infty L^4}
\leq K_T.
\]
Applying the $L^4$ to $L^\infty$ gradient heat estimate to the
concentration mild formula, we conclude that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-scalar-Linfty}
\sup_{0<t<S}\bigl(\|n(t)\|_{L^\infty}
+\|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^\infty}\bigr)\leq K_T.
\end{equation}

It remains to control the other Sobolev norms in the fixed-point space.
The cell equation reads
\[
n_t-\Delta n=-(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla n-a_\tau'(n)\nabla n\cdot\nabla c
-a_\tau(n)\Delta c.
\]
Since $0\leq a_\tau'(n)\leq1$, we have
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\|(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla n+a_\tau'(n)\nabla n\cdot\nabla c+a_\tau(n)\Delta c\|_{L^2L^2}\\
&\leq\bigl(\|(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}
+\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\bigr)
\|\nabla n\|_{L^2L^2}
+\tau^{-1}\|\Delta c\|_{L^2L^2}\\
&\leq K_T.
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:Neumann-high-order-estimate} with $m=0$ to $n$ gives
\[
\|n\|_{L^\infty H^1}+\|n\|_{L^2H^2}
+\|n_t\|_{L^2L^2}\leq K_T.
\]

Finally, $0\leq h_\tau'(n)\leq1$ and $h_\tau(n)\leq n$ give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\nabla \bigl(u\cdot\nabla c+h_\tau(n)c\bigr)\|_{L^2L^2}
\leq{}&S^{1/2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^\infty L^4}
\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty L^4}
+\|u\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|D^2c\|_{L^2L^2}\\
&+\|c\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|\nabla n\|_{L^2L^2}
+\|n\|_{L^\infty L^\infty}\|\nabla c\|_{L^2L^2}
\leq K_T.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus $\|\bigl(u\cdot\nabla c+h_\tau(n)c\bigr)\|_{L^2H^1}\leq K_T$. Since
$c_0^\varepsilon\in H_N^2$, applying
\eqref{eq:Neumann-high-order-estimate} with $m=1$ yields
\[
\|c\|_{L^\infty H^2}+\|c\|_{L^2H^3}
+\|c_t\|_{L^2H^1}\leq K_T.
\]
The standard time-continuity part of the linear regularity estimates
gives $n\in C([0,S];H^1)$ and $c\in C([0,S];H_N^2)$.
The mild formulas give continuity in the remaining fixed-point
norms: their time singularities are integrable, and the regularized
initial data are smooth and boundary compatible.

We have therefore bounded every component of
\eqref{eq:fixed-point-norm}, as well as all the norms in
\eqref{eq:fixed-parameter-high-order}, by constants independent of
$S<\min\{T,T_*\}$. If $T_*<\infty$, choose $T>T_*$ and let
$S\uparrow T_*$. The local contraction argument can then be
restarted at any $t_0<T_*$ with an existence time bounded below by
a positive constant depending only on the preceding uniform bounds
and the fixed parameters. Choosing $t_0$ sufficiently close to
$T_*$ extends the solution beyond $T_*$; uniqueness ensures agreement
with the original solution on their common interval. This
contradicts the maximality of $T_*$. Hence $T_*=\infty$, and
\eqref{eq:fixed-parameter-high-order} follows for every finite $T$.
These strong-solution bounds, together with spatial cutoffs and
time regularization, also justify the subsequent energy calculations.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:uniform-energy}
Let $(n_0^\varepsilon,c_0^\varepsilon,u_0^\varepsilon)$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
& n_0^\varepsilon \in L^1,\qquad
(n_0^\varepsilon+1)\log(n_0^\varepsilon+1)\in L^1,
\qquad u_0^\varepsilon\in L_\sigma^2,\\
& \nabla\sqrt{\tilde{c_0}^\varepsilon}\in L^2,\qquad
c_0^\varepsilon\in L^1\cap L^\infty,\\
& n_0^\varepsilon\geq 0,\qquad c_0^\varepsilon\geq 0.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}

Assume that $(n^{\varepsilon,\tau},c^{\varepsilon,\tau},
u^{\varepsilon,\tau})$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:regularized-half-space-system}.
Then there exists a constant $C>0$ independent of
$\varepsilon$ and $\tau$ such that for every \(T<\infty\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:uniform-energy}
\begin{aligned}
&\sup_{0<t<T}\left\{\int_{\mathbb H^3}n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)+H(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t))\,dx
+\|\nabla \sqrt{\tilde{c}^{\varepsilon,\tau}}(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+\|u^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^2}^2\right\}\\
&\quad+\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}\frac{|\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}|^2}{1+n^{\varepsilon,\tau}}\,dxdt
+\int_0^T\|\nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde{c}^{\varepsilon,\tau}}\|_{L^2}^2\,dt
+\int_0^T\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2\,dt\\
&\quad+\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}(\sqrt{\tilde{c}^{\varepsilon,\tau}})^{-2}|\nabla \sqrt{\tilde{c}^{\varepsilon,\tau}}|^4\,dxdt
+\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})|\nabla \sqrt{\tilde{c}^{\varepsilon,\tau}}|^2\,dxdt\\
&\leq C_{\rm in}(1+T),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the constant is independent of \(\varepsilon\) and \(\tau\). Since \(\sqrt{\tilde{c}^{\varepsilon,\tau}}=\sqrt{1+c^{\varepsilon,\tau}}\geq1\), every negative power in \eqref{eq:uniform-energy} is bounded and no \(c+\delta\) regularization is required.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We omit the approximation indices and recall that
$H(n)=(1+n)\log(1+n)$. The entropy test gives
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\int H(n)
+4\int|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}|^2
=\int\frac{n h_\tau'(n)}{1+n}\nabla n\cdot\nabla c.
\]
Use \eqref{eq:approx-c-combined}, proved below by integrations by parts,
with cutoffs tending to one, multiply it by $4/7$, and estimate the
fluid coupling by \eqref{eq:approx-fluid-coupling}. Adding the velocity
energy identity multiplied by $112M_c$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
&\frac{d}{dt}\int
\bigl(H(n)+2|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2+112M_c|u|^2\bigr)
\\ &\qquad+4\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2
+\frac47\|D^2\sqrt{1+c}\|_{L^2}^2
+2\int h_\tau(n)|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2\\
&\qquad
+\frac14\int\frac{|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^4}{1+c}
+112M_c\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2
\\
&\leq\int\frac{4h_\tau'(n)(n-c)}{\sqrt{1+n}\sqrt{1+c}}
\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\cdot\nabla\sqrt{1+c}\\
&\qquad
-2\int\frac{h_\tau(n)}{1+c}|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2
-224M_c\int \mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\cdot u.
\end{align*}
In particular, the factor $n/(1+n)$ in the entropy equation is retained.
Since $0\leq h_\tau'(n)\leq1$,
$n(h_\tau'(n))^2\leq h_\tau(n)$ and
$(n-c)^2/(1+n)\leq n+c^2$, Young's inequality gives
\begin{align*}
&\int\frac{4h_\tau'(n)(n-c)}{\sqrt{1+n}\sqrt{1+c}}
\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\cdot\nabla\sqrt{1+c}
-2\int\frac{h_\tau(n)}{1+c}|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2\\
&\leq2\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2\\
&\quad+2\int
\frac{(h_\tau'(n))^2(n-c)^2/(1+n)-h_\tau(n)}{1+c}
|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2\\
&\leq2\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2
+2\int\frac{c^2}{1+c}|\nabla\sqrt{1+c}|^2\\
&\leq2\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2
+\tfrac12\|\nabla c\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{align*}

For the force, mass conservation gives
\[
\|\sqrt{1+n}-1\|_{L^2}^2\leq\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1},
\qquad
\nabla(\sqrt{1+n}-1)=\nabla\sqrt{1+n}.
\]
Splitting at $n=1$, applying the half-space Gagliardo--Nirenberg
inequality to $\sqrt{1+n}-1$, and interpolating between $L^1$
and $L^{5/3}$ yield
\[
\begin{aligned}
\int n^{5/3}
&\leq C\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}^{2/3}
\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2
+C\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1},\\
\|n\|_{L^{6/5}}^2
&\leq\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}^{7/6}
\left(\int n^{5/3}\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Consequently, for each $\eta>0$,
\[
\|n\|_{L^{6/5}}^2
\leq\eta\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2
+C_{\eta,\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}}.
\]
Sobolev embedding and the $L^{6/5}$-boundedness of
$\mathcal K_\varepsilon$ therefore imply
\[
224M_c\left|\int \mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\cdot u\right|
\leq56M_c\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2
+\|\nabla\sqrt{1+n}\|_{L^2}^2+C_{\rm in}.
\]
Finally, the concentration $L^2$ identity gives
\[
\int_0^T\|\nabla c\|_{L^2}^2\,dt
\leq\frac12\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
Integration, mass conservation, and
\eqref{eq:initial-data-uniform-control} prove \eqref{eq:uniform-energy},
after enlarging $C_{\rm in}$. Every constant in this argument is
independent of $\varepsilon$ and $\tau$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:uniform-pressure}
Let
\[
0<\sigma<T<\infty,
\]
and let $D\subset\mathbb H^3$ be a bounded Lipschitz domain whose closure
is compact in $\overline{\mathbb H^3}$. If $\overline D\cap\Gamma\neq\varnothing$,
we assume that the corresponding portion of $\partial D$ agrees locally
with the flat boundary $\Gamma$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:pressure-five-thirds}
\left\|
p^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}((\sigma,T)\times D)}
\leq
C_{\sigma,D,C_{\rm in}}(1+T),
\end{equation}
where
\[
[p^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
:=
\fint_D p^{\varepsilon,\tau}(x,t)\,dx,
\]
and the constant is independent of $\varepsilon$ and $\tau$.
No uniform $L^{5/3}$ estimate for the pressure at the genuine initial
time $t=0$ is asserted.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:global-G-bound},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:G-global-pressure}
\left\|
\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\nabla\phi)\bigr)
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{15/14}(\mathbb H^3))}
\leq
C_{\rm in}(1+T).
\end{equation}

The crucial point is that the Stokes decomposition is performed on
the whole half-space, before any spatial localization.  This avoids
using the nonlocal Helmholtz projection on data known only in a
bounded subdomain.

Let
\((v^{\varepsilon,\tau},p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau})\)
be the solution of the zero-initial-data Stokes problem
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:global-pressure-stokes-forced}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
\partial_t v^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-\Delta v^{\varepsilon,\tau}
+\nabla p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}
&=
-\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\nabla\phi)\bigr)
&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\mathbb H^3,
\\
\nabla\cdot v^{\varepsilon,\tau}&=0
&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\mathbb H^3,
\\
v^{\varepsilon,\tau}&=0
&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\Gamma,
\\
v^{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot,0)&=0
&&\text{in }\mathbb H^3.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}
By the maximal $L^{5/3}_tL^{15/14}_x$-regularity of the
Dirichlet Stokes system in the half-space
(see \cite[Theorem~6.3 and the subsequent discussion,
p.~136]{DHP2001}), we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:global-stokes-maximal-pressure}
\begin{aligned}
&
\left\|
\partial_t v^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{15/14})}
+
\left\|
\nabla^2 v^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{15/14})}
\\
&\qquad
+
\left\|
\nabla p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{15/14})}\\
&\leq
C
\left\|
\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\nabla\phi)\bigr)
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{15/14})}
\leq
C_{\rm in}(1+T),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the spatial norms in this estimate are over
\(\mathbb H^3\).

Since $14/15-1/3=3/5$,
the Sobolev--Poincar\'e inequality on the bounded domain \(D\) gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:p1-local-five-thirds}
\left\|
p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{5/3}(D))}
\leq
C_D
\left\|
\nabla p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(0,T;L^{15/14}(\mathbb H^3))}
\leq
C_{D,C_{\rm in}}(1+T).
\end{equation}


For fixed $\varepsilon,\tau>0$, the strong-solution estimates
also give $\bigl((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}+\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\nabla\phi)\bigr)\in L^2(0,T;L^2(\mathbb H^3))$ for the forcing
in the Stokes problem above. The Stokes semigroups and Helmholtz
projections are consistent on intersections of their finite-$L^r$
spaces \cite{DHP2001}. Consequently the zero-initial-data solution
$v^{\varepsilon,\tau}$ constructed by mixed-norm maximal regularity
coincides with the $L^2$ Stokes solution. This permits the following
identification in the global energy class.

We next isolate the part generated by the initial velocity.  Define
\[
w^{\varepsilon,\tau}
:=
u^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
v^{\varepsilon,\tau},
\qquad
p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}
:=
p^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}.
\]
Then
\((w^{\varepsilon,\tau},p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau})\)
solves the homogeneous Stokes system
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:global-homogeneous-stokes-pressure}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
\partial_t w^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-\Delta w^{\varepsilon,\tau}
+\nabla p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}
&=0
&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\mathbb H^3,
\\
\nabla\cdot w^{\varepsilon,\tau}&=0,
\\
w^{\varepsilon,\tau}&=0
&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\Gamma,
\\
w^{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot,0)
&=
u_0^\varepsilon.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}
Thus
\[
w^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)
=
e^{-tA}u_0^\varepsilon,
\]
with the same Dirichlet Stokes operator $A$ on $L_\sigma^2(\mathbb H^3)$.

Analyticity of the Stokes semigroup gives
\[
\left\|
Ae^{-tA}u_0^\varepsilon
\right\|_{L^2}
\leq
Ct^{-1}
\left\|u_0^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^2},
\qquad t>0.
\]
Together with the half-space stationary Stokes estimate, this yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:homogeneous-pressure-decay}
\left\|
\nabla p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)
\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb H^3)}
\leq
Ct^{-1}
\left\|u_0^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^2},
\qquad t>0.
\end{equation}
Therefore, since \(D\) is bounded,
\begin{align}
&
\left\|
p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(\sigma,T;L^{5/3}(D))}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq
C_D
\left\|
\nabla p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(\sigma,T;L^{15/14}(D))}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq
C_D
\left\|
\nabla p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}
\right\|_
{L^{5/3}(\sigma,T;L^2(\mathbb H^3))}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq
C_D
\left(
\int_\sigma^T t^{-5/3}\,dt
\right)^{3/5}
\left\|u_0^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^2}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq
C_{\sigma,D,C_{\rm in}}.
\label{eq:p2-local-five-thirds}
\end{align}

Finally,
\[
p^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
=
\left(
p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p_1^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
\right)
+
\left(
p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p_2^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)
\right).
\]
Combining
\eqref{eq:p1-local-five-thirds}
and
\eqref{eq:p2-local-five-thirds}
proves
\eqref{eq:pressure-five-thirds}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{rem:pressure-away-initial}
For every $0<\sigma<T<\infty$, Proposition~\ref{prop:uniform-pressure}
provides a uniform local pressure estimate on the time interval
$(\sigma,T)$, with a constant that may depend on $\sigma$ but is
independent of the regularization parameters $\varepsilon$ and $\tau$.
Consequently, for every compact interval
$I\Subset(0,\infty)$ and every bounded domain
$D\subset\mathbb H^3$ whose closure is compact in
$\overline{\mathbb H^3}$, the normalized pressures $p^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-
[p^{\varepsilon,\tau}]_D(t)$ are weakly
compact in $L^{5/3}(I\times D)$.
This local-in-time pressure control is sufficient for the subsequent
compactness and regularity arguments, since all distributional test
functions and all test functions entering the local energy inequality
are supported away from the genuine initial time $t=0$.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:approximate-local-energy}
To keep the displayed inequality compact, only in this lemma and its proof we omit the superscript \((\varepsilon,\tau)\) and write
\[
\begin{aligned}
(n,c,u,p)&=(n^{\varepsilon,\tau},c^{\varepsilon,\tau},u^{\varepsilon,\tau},p^{\varepsilon,\tau}),
&\qquad \tilde c&=1+c,\\
a&=a_\tau(n),\qquad h=h_\tau(n).
\end{aligned}
\]
For almost every \(t>0\),
\begin{align}
&\int_{\mathbb H^3}\!\big[n\log n+2|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2+112M_c|u|^2\big]\psi(t)\,dx \nonumber\\
&\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb H^3}\!\Big[2|\nabla\sqrt n|^2+\frac47|\nabla^2\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
+2h|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
+\frac14\tilde c^{-1}|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^4
+112M_c|\nabla u|^2\Big]\psi\,dxds \nonumber\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb H^3}\!\Big[n\log n\big(\partial_s\psi+\Delta\psi+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla\psi\big)
+a(1+\log n)\nabla c\cdot\nabla\psi \nonumber\\
&\quad+2|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
\big(\partial_s\psi+\Delta\psi+u\cdot\nabla\psi\big) \nonumber\\
&\quad-\frac47\tilde c^{-1/2}|\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}|^2
\nabla\sqrt{\tilde c}\cdot\nabla\psi
-\frac{16}{7}\tilde c^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^3
(\partial_i\sqrt{\tilde c})^3\partial_i\psi \nonumber\\
&\quad+112M_c\Big\{|u|^2
\big(\partial_s\psi+\Delta\psi+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla\psi\big)
\nonumber\\
&\qquad+2(p-\overline p(s))u\cdot\nabla\psi-2\mathcal K_\varepsilon(n\nabla\phi)\cdot u\,\psi\Big\}\Big]\,dxds.
\label{eq:approximate-local-energy}
  \end{align}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We keep every modification caused by the regularization. In this proof we suppress \((\varepsilon,\tau)\) and write
\[
a=a_\tau(n),\qquad h=h_\tau(n),\qquad \tilde c=1+c.
\]
We use \(\iint\) for integration over \((0,t)\times\mathbb H^3\) and \(\iint_\Gamma\) for integration over \((0,t)\times\Gamma\). The calculation is first performed with a compact spatial cutoff, so the only boundary that remains is \(\Gamma\). Its outward normal is \(\nu=-e_3\). On \(\Gamma\),
\[
(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)=u=0,\qquad \partial_\nu n=\partial_\nu c=\partial_\nu \sqrt{\tilde{c}}=0,
\qquad \partial_\nu\psi=0.
\]
Since the solution is smooth, \(\partial_{i3}\sqrt{\tilde{c}}=\partial_i(\partial_3\sqrt{\tilde{c}})=0\) on \(\Gamma\) for \(i=1,2\). We do not suppress any boundary integral below: each one is displayed and then evaluated by these conditions. Notice also that \(\sqrt{\tilde{c}}\geq1\). Therefore the factors \(\sqrt{\tilde{c}}^{-1}\) and \(\sqrt{\tilde{c}}^{-2}\) are bounded and the concentration calculation contains no singular negative power.

\emph{The cell equation.} Multiply
\[
\partial_tn+(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla n-\Delta n+\nabla\cdot(a\nabla c)=0
\]
by \((1+\log n)\psi\). If \(n\) can vanish, replace \(\log n\) by \(\log(n+\delta)\) and let \(\delta\downarrow0\) at the end. At the $\delta$-level the chemotactic term contains the factor
\[
\frac{n}{n+\delta}h_\tau'(n),
\]
rather than $h_\tau'(n)$.  Since
\[
0\leq\frac{n}{n+\delta}\leq1
\]
and
\[
\frac{n}{n+\delta}\longrightarrow1
\qquad\text{a.e. on }\{n>0\},
\]
all identities used below are first derived at fixed $\delta>0$,
and the displayed cancellation is recovered after letting
$\delta\downarrow0$ by dominated convergence.

Since \(\psi(\cdot,0)=0\), the time term is
\[
\iint\partial_sn\,(1+\log n)\psi=\int_{\mathbb H^3}n\log n\,\psi(t)\,dx-\iint n\log n\,\partial_s\psi.
\]
For the transport term,
\begin{align*}
&\iint (\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla(n\log n)\,\psi\\
&=\iint_\Gamma n\log n\,\psi\,(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nu
-\iint n\log n\,(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla\psi\\
&=-\iint n\log n\,(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla\psi,
\end{align*}
because \((\mathcal J_\varepsilon u)=0\) on \(\Gamma\). For the diffusion term,
\begin{align*}
&-\iint\Delta n\,(1+\log n)\psi\\
&={}-\iint_\Gamma\partial_\nu n(1+\log n)\psi
+\iint\frac{|\nabla n|^2}{n}\psi
+\iint(1+\log n)\nabla n\cdot\nabla\psi\\
