-h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau}).
\]
For a fixed terminal time $t$, reversing time in the backward
adjoint equation therefore replaces the transport velocity by
$-u^{\varepsilon,\tau}$, with both coefficients evaluated at the
reversed time. This velocity is still divergence free and vanishes
on $\Gamma$, and the absorption coefficient is still nonnegative.
The same $L^1$ contraction and Nash argument apply to the adjoint
solution operator $U(t,s)^*$. Hence, by duality,
\[
\|U(t,s)\|_{L^2\to L^\infty}
=\|U(t,s)^*\|_{L^1\to L^2}
\leq C(t-s)^{-3/4}.
\]
The evolution property
$U(t,s)=U(t,(s+t)/2)U((s+t)/2,s)$ now yields
\[
\|U(t,s)\|_{L^1\to L^\infty}\leq C(t-s)^{-3/2}.
\]
Taking $s=0$ and $f=c_0^\varepsilon$, we recover
$c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)=U(t,0)c_0^\varepsilon$ and use
$\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}\leq L$ to obtain the decay bound in
\eqref{eq:ev-signal-decay}. The maximum principle and
$\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^\infty}\leq\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}$ give
its other bound.

Finally, testing the regularized concentration equation by
$(c^{\varepsilon,\tau})^{q-1}$, for $2\leq q<\infty$, gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dt}\|c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^q}^q
+\frac{4(q-1)}q
\|\nabla(c^{\varepsilon,\tau})^{q/2}\|_{L^2}^2
+q\int_{\mathbb H^3}h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})
(c^{\varepsilon,\tau})^q\,dx=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Dropping the nonnegative absorption term proves
\eqref{eq:ev-signal-energy}. None of these bounds uses a norm of
the transport velocity. Almost-everywhere convergence on an
exhaustion of $\mathbb H^3\times(0,\infty)$ passes
\eqref{eq:ev-signal-decay} to the successive limits.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:ev-selection}
The approximation in Section~\ref{sec:existence} admits a diagonal
subsequence selected by first letting $\varepsilon\to0$ for each fixed
$\tau>0$, and then letting $\tau\to0$, whose limit has all the properties
asserted in Theorem~\ref{thm:half-space-existence}.
In addition, the velocity satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-global-kinetic}
 \frac12\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^2+\int_s^t\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2
 \le \frac12\|u(s)\|_{L^2}^2
       -\int_s^t\int_{\mathbb H^3} n\nabla\phi\cdot u
\end{equation}
for $s=0$ and for almost every $s>0$, and every $t\ge s$.

At the intermediate, fixed-$\tau$ level, the scalar equations have the
same transport velocity $u^\tau$, and, for every $0<\delta<T<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-fixed-tau-scalar-class}
\begin{aligned}
 &n^\tau\in L^\infty((\delta,T);L^1\cap L^4)
       \cap L^2((\delta,T);H^1),\\
 &c^\tau\in L^\infty(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)),\qquad
 \nabla c^\tau\in L^4(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The constants in this assertion may depend on $\delta,T,\tau$.
The density bounds in \eqref{eq:eventual-fixed-tau-scalar-class}
are asserted for $n^\tau$ at fixed $\tau$, not as uniform bounds for
the final density $n$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
The local convergence and localized energy inequality follow from
Section~\ref{sec:existence}. We explain the additional estimates
in three steps: fixed-$\tau$ scalar regularity, density tightness,
and global velocity compactness. These retain the scalar tests
and the kinetic-energy inequality for the same selected limit.

\emph{Step 1: scalar regularity before removing the truncation.}
Fix $\tau>0$ and retain the superscript $(\varepsilon,\tau)$ throughout
this step. All estimates below are uniform in $\varepsilon$ at this
fixed $\tau$; the density bounds may depend on $\tau$.
Testing the concentration equation with $c^{\varepsilon,\tau}$ gives
\[
 \sup_{t<T}\|c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 +2\int_0^T\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2
 \le\|c_0\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
Since $a_\tau\le\tau^{-1}$, testing the density equation with
$n^{\varepsilon,\tau}$ gives
\[
 \frac{d}{dt}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2
 +\|\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2
 \le\tau^{-2}\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
The half-space Nash inequality and
$\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^1}=M$ imply
\[
 \frac{d}{dt}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2
 +c_M\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^{10/3}
 \le\tau^{-2}\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
Here and below $c_M>0$ depends only on $M$ and dimensional constants.
Compare the squared $L^2$ norm with the solution of the
corresponding unforced differential equation plus the time integral
of the nonnegative forcing. This sum is a supersolution, and
integration of the unforced equation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-forced-ode-comparison}
 \|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 \le C_Mt^{-3/2}+C\tau^{-2}\|c_0\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{equation}
The estimate is independent of the approximate initial $L^2$ norm.
Integrating the preceding density-energy inequality from a positive
time gives, for $0<\delta<T$,
\[
 \sup_{\delta<t<T}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 +\int_\delta^T\|\nabla n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2}^2
 \le C_{\delta,T,\tau}.
\]
No uniform bound for $\|n_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^2}$ is used.

For the additional $L^4$ regularity, testing with
$(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})^3$ gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{d}{dt}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^4
 +\frac32\|\nabla(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})^2\|_{L^2}^2
 \le
 6\tau^{-2}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^2
       \|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
Mass interpolation and the Sobolev inequality give
\[
 \|\nabla(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})^2\|_{L^2}^2
 \ge c_M\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^{44/9}.
\]
Dividing the resulting inequality by
$2\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^2$, with positive regularization
at its zeros, gives
\[
 \frac{d}{dt}\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^2
 +c_M\|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^{26/9}
 \le C\tau^{-2}\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^2.
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:uniform-energy} gives
\[
 \int_0^T\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^2
 \le T^{1/2}
       \left(\int_0^T\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^4}^4\right)^{1/2}
 \le C_T,
\]
with $C_T$ independent of both parameters. Applying the same
differential-inequality comparison as in
\eqref{eq:eventual-forced-ode-comparison} to the squared $L^4$ norm yields
\[
 \|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^4}^2
 \le C_Mt^{-9/4}+C_T\tau^{-2},\qquad 0<t<T.
\]
In particular,
\[
 \sup_{0<\varepsilon<1}
 \|n^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^\infty((\delta,T);L^4)}
 \le C_{\delta,T,\tau}.
\]
We now let $\varepsilon\to0$ at this fixed $\tau$. Weak lower
semicontinuity and the local strong convergence from
Section~\ref{sec:existence} give
\eqref{eq:eventual-fixed-tau-scalar-class} for
$(n^\tau,c^\tau,u^\tau)$.
The approximation property of $\mathcal J_\varepsilon$ gives
\[
 \|\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau}
             -u^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2(0,T;L^2)}
 \le C\varepsilon^{1/2}
       \|\nabla u^{\varepsilon,\tau}\|_{L^2(0,T;L^2)}
 \longrightarrow0.
\]
Consequently the limiting scalar equations are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \partial_t n^\tau+u^\tau\cdot\nabla n^\tau-\Delta n^\tau
   &=-\nabla\cdot\bigl(a_\tau(n^\tau)\nabla c^\tau\bigr),\\
 \partial_t c^\tau+u^\tau\cdot\nabla c^\tau-\Delta c^\tau
   &=-h_\tau(n^\tau)c^\tau.
\end{aligned}
\]
The $L^4$ and $L^2H^1$ density bounds in this step are only
fixed-$\tau$ bounds. They justify the later scalar tests at that
level; they are not passed directly to the final density $n$
as $\tau\to0$.

\emph{Step 2: tightness of density and convergence of the force.}

For the cutoff estimates in this step, suppress the superscripts
$(\varepsilon,\tau)$. The bounds from Section~\ref{sec:existence}
are uniform in both parameters on each finite time interval:
\[
 n\in L^\infty_tL^1_x\cap L^{5/3}_{t,x},\qquad
 u,\mathcal J_\varepsilon u,\nabla c\in L^2_tL^6_x.
\]
We use these bounds here, rather than the fixed-$\tau$ estimates
from Step~1.
For the last assertion, note explicitly that
\[
 D^2c=2\sqrt{1+c}\,D^2\sqrt{1+c}
       +2\nabla\sqrt{1+c}\otimes\nabla\sqrt{1+c}
       \in L^2_{t,x},
\]
and use the half-space Sobolev inequality for $\nabla c$.
Mass interpolation also gives $n\in L^2_tL^{6/5}_x$.
Take a radial exterior cutoff $\eta_R$, equal to zero on $B_R$ and
one outside $B_{2R}$, with $|D^j\eta_R|\le CR^{-j}$ and
$\partial_\nu\eta_R=0$ on $\Gamma$.  The scalar equation and
$a_\tau(n)\le n$ yield
\begin{align*}
 \sup_{0<t<T}\int_{\mathbb H^3} n(t)\eta_R
 &\le \int_{\mathbb H^3} n_0^\varepsilon\eta_R+\frac{CMT}{R^2}
       +\frac C R\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}n(|\mathcal J_\varepsilon u|+|\nabla c|)\\
 &\le \int_{\mathbb H^3} n_0^\varepsilon\eta_R+\frac{C_T}{R}.
\end{align*}
Thus mass is uniformly tight in space.
Combining this fact with local strong convergence gives
$n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}\to n^\tau$ at fixed $\tau$, and subsequently
$n^{\tau_j}\to n$, strongly in $L^r(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T))$
for every $1\le r<5/3$.
To include times near zero, interpolate the uniformly bounded
$L^{5/3}_{t,x}$ norm with the $L^1$ norm on $\mathbb H^3\times(0,\delta)$,
which is at most $M\delta$; this makes each such $L^r$ norm
uniformly small as $\delta\downarrow0$.
More precisely, interpolation
on a measurable set $E\subset\mathbb H^3$ gives
\[
 \int_0^T\|n\mathbf1_E\|_{L^{6/5}}^2
 \le C_T\bigl(\sup_{t<T}\|n(t)\mathbf1_E\|_{L^1}\bigr)^{7/6}
             \left(\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3} n^{5/3}\right)^{1/2}.
\]

On bounded sets, the same convergence is strong in
$L^2_tL^{6/5}_x$. Indeed, the interpolation inequality
\[
 \|f(t)\|_{L^{6/5}}^2
 \leq\|f(t)\|_{L^1}\|f(t)\|_{L^{3/2}},
\]
applied to the difference of a density and its limit, combines the
uniform $L^\infty_tL^1_x$ bound with strong $L^{3/2}_{t,x}$
convergence. The preceding tail estimate extends this convergence
to the whole half-space at each of the two limiting stages.

Thus, with all convergences strong in
$L^2((0,T);L^{6/5}(\mathbb H^3))$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-global-force-compactness}
\begin{aligned}
 n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}&\longrightarrow n^\tau,&
 f^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}&\longrightarrow n^\tau\nabla\phi
 &&(\tau\text{ fixed}),\\
 n^{\tau_j}&\longrightarrow n,&
 n^{\tau_j}\nabla\phi&\longrightarrow n\nabla\phi
 &&(\tau_j\downarrow0).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For the first force convergence, use
$f^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}=\mathcal K_{\varepsilon_j}
(n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}\nabla\phi)$, boundedness and strong continuity
of $\mathcal K_\varepsilon$ on $L^{6/5}$, and
$\nabla\phi\in L^\infty$. The second passage uses the same uniform
density-tail estimates and has no force-smoothing operator.

\emph{Step 3: global velocity compactness, including the initial time.}

At the first passage, fix $\tau$ and write
\[
 (n_j,u_j,b_j,p_j,f_j)
 =(n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau},u^{\varepsilon_j,\tau},
   \mathcal J_{\varepsilon_j}u^{\varepsilon_j,\tau},
   p^{\varepsilon_j,\tau},f^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}).
\]
At the second passage the corresponding sequence is
\[
 (n_j,u_j,b_j,p_j,f_j)
 =(n^{\tau_j},u^{\tau_j},u^{\tau_j},p^{\tau_j},
   n^{\tau_j}\nabla\phi).
\]
Only within Step~3, unindexed solution variables denote the limit of
the sequence currently under consideration: the fixed-$\tau$ limit
in the first passage and the final selected limit in the second.
The initial velocity is $u_0^{\varepsilon_j}$ in the first passage
and exactly $u_0$ in the second. All constants used below are
uniform in the remaining approximation parameters.

We include this step because local strong convergence of velocity alone
would not suffice to pass the kinetic energy at almost every starting
time.  The global Stokes decomposition in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:uniform-pressure} permits the normalization
\[
 p_j=p_{1j}+p_{2j},\qquad
 \|p_{1j}\|_{L^{5/3}(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T))}\le C_T.
\]
Indeed, $\nabla p_{1j}\in L^{5/3}_tL^{15/14}_x$, and the homogeneous
Sobolev inequality on $\mathbb H^3$ selects the unique representative in
$L^{5/3}(\mathbb H^3)$.  At the first limit, the second part is the pressure generated by
$e^{-tA}u_0^{\varepsilon_j}$; choose its representative in
$L^6(\mathbb H^3)$. At the second limit it is the same pressure
generated by $e^{-tA}u_0$ for every $j$. Thus, for every $0<\delta<T$,
the first limit satisfies
\[
 \|p_{2j}(t)\|_{L^6}\le C t^{-1}\|u_0^{\varepsilon_j}\|_{L^2},
 \qquad
 p_{2j}\longrightarrow p_2
       \quad\hbox{in }L^1((\delta,T);L^6(\mathbb H^3)).
\]
The latter convergence follows from linearity and
$u_0^{\varepsilon_j}\to u_0$ in $L^2$.

There is uniform strong continuity at the genuine initial time along
the approximation sequence.  In fact, interpolation gives
\[
 \int_0^t\|n_j\|_{L^{6/5}}^2
 \le C t^{1/2}
      \left(\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}n_j^{5/3}\right)^{1/2}
 \le C t^{1/2}\qquad(0<t<1).
\]
The kinetic-energy identity, or the inequality retained after the
first limit, and Young's inequality imply
\[
 \|u_j(t)\|_{L^2}^2\le\|u_j(0)\|_{L^2}^2+C t^{1/2}.
\]
The equations also bound $\partial_tu_j$ uniformly in
\[
 L^{4/3}\bigl(0,T;
 (H^1_0(\mathbb H^3)\cap L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3))'\bigr).
\]
The convection term is controlled by
$b_j,u_j\in L^{8/3}_tL^4_x$, and the force by
\eqref{eq:eventual-global-force-compactness}. Testing against fixed
solenoidal $H^1_0$ functions and using their density in
$L^2_\sigma$ proves uniform weak continuity at zero.  Together with the last
upper bound on the norm, this proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-uniform-initial-continuity}
 \lim_{\delta\downarrow0}\limsup_{j\to\infty}
       \sup_{0<t<\delta}\|u_j(t)-u_0\|_{L^2}=0.
\end{equation}

For the exterior kinetic-energy test, first multiply $\eta_R$ by
$1-\eta_{2^mR}$ and then let $m\to\infty$. The energy and convection
bounds and the global pressure bounds just established remove the
outer cutoff errors; the $p_{2j}$ error is controlled by its
$L^1((\delta,T);L^6)$ spatial tail, as below.
Apply the approximate local kinetic-energy identity with the exterior
cutoff $\eta_R$ on $(\delta,t)$, choosing $\delta$ from the
full-measure set of admissible initial times when working with a limit.  Diffusion and convection cutoff errors
are bounded by
\[
 CR^{-2}\int_\delta^T\|u_j\|_{L^2}^2
 +CR^{-1}\int_\delta^T\int_{\mathbb H^3} |b_j||u_j|^2=o_R(1)
\]
uniformly in $j$.  The $p_{1j}$ contribution is at most
\[
 CR^{-1}\|p_{1j}\|_{L^{5/3}_{t,x}}
           \|u_j\|_{L^{5/2}_{t,x}}=o_R(1).
\]
Since the cutoff annulus has volume at most $CR^3$, the remaining pressure
term is bounded by
\[
 C\|u_j\|_{L^\infty_tL^2_x}
    \int_\delta^T\|p_{2j}(t)\|_{L^6(\mathbb H^3\setminus B_R)}\,dt=o_R(1).
\]
Here uniformity follows from the strong convergence of $p_{2j}$ on
$(\delta,T)$.  Finally the force term is bounded by
\[
 C\|f_j\|_{L^2((\delta,T);L^{6/5}(\mathbb H^3\setminus B_R))}
     \|u_j\|_{L^2((\delta,T);L^6)}=o_R(1)
\]
by \eqref{eq:eventual-global-force-compactness}.  Consequently,
\[
 \sup_{\delta<t<T}\int_{\mathbb H^3\setminus B_{2R}}|u_j(t)|^2
 \le C\int_{\mathbb H^3}|u_j(\delta)|^2\eta_R+o_R(1).
\]
For each fixed admissible $\delta>0$, take first $\limsup_{j\to\infty}$
and then $R\to\infty$. The initial tail is bounded by
$C\limsup_j\|u_j(\delta)-u_0\|_{L^2}^2$, since $u_0\in L^2$.
The part $0<t<\delta$ is controlled directly by
\eqref{eq:eventual-uniform-initial-continuity}. Letting $\delta\downarrow0$
through common admissible times proves the precise tightness statement
\[
 \lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{j\to\infty}
 \operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{0<t<T}
 \int_{\mathbb H^3\setminus B_R}|u_j(t)|^2=0.
\]
At the second limit, the homogeneous pressure is already the same
pressure generated by $e^{-tA}u_0$ for every $\tau$.
The time-derivative bound also selects a representative in
$C_{\rm w}([0,T];L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3))$ for each limit.
Local compactness therefore improves to global strong convergence in
$L^2(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T))$.  A subsequence converges strongly in $L^2(\mathbb H^3)$ at
almost every time.

We may now pass to the kinetic-energy inequality from each such starting
time $s$: the force work converges by
\eqref{eq:eventual-global-force-compactness} and weak convergence of
$u_j$ in $L^2_tL^6_x$, the energy at $s$ converges strongly, and the
terminal energy and dissipation are lower semicontinuous.  Weak continuity
of the limit extends the inequality to every terminal time $t\ge s$.
At $s=0$ use strong convergence of the initial data.  This argument applies
first at fixed $\tau$ and then to a countable sequence $\tau\downarrow0$.
The same passage also retains the local kinetic-energy inequality for
the fixed-$\tau$ velocity: the pressure--velocity product converges by
weak $L^{5/3}$ convergence of pressure and strong local $L^{5/2}$
convergence of velocity.  Thus the exterior-cutoff argument remains
available at the second limit, even though its input is then an
inequality instead of the smooth identity.  Intersecting the
corresponding full-measure sets of starting times proves
\eqref{eq:eventual-global-kinetic} for the final limit.

Finally, realize the iterated limit $(n,c,u,p)$ by a diagonal
sequence of the original smooth approximants.
For a convergent sequence $\tau_j\downarrow0$, choose
$\varepsilon_j<1/j$ so that the original approximant is within $1/j$
of the fixed-$\tau_j$ limit in the global strong norms above and in
the strong local norms of Section~\ref{subsec:compactness-local-energy}
on the first $j$ members of a fixed compact exhaustion. Impose the
same accuracy for pressure pairings against the first $j$ members
of countable dense families of dual tests. These finitely many
requirements can be met simultaneously. This diagonal sequence has
the same limit in all the topologies used in that section, so its
original localized entropy--energy argument proves suitability of
this precise limit. The additional global and scalar estimates are
retained for the same selection.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:ev-scalar-decay}
For the selection in Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection},
\[
 \|c(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\le C t^{-3/2}\|c_0\|_{L^1}.
\]
There exists $T_0<\infty$ such that, for $q=2,6$ and almost every
$t\ge T_0+1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-density-decay}
 \|n(t)\|_{L^q}\le C_q M(t-T_0)^{-\frac32(1-1/q)}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every $t\geq T_0+1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-density-tail}
 \int_t^\infty\|\nabla n(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds
       \le C M^2(t-T_0)^{-3/2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-signal} supplies the stated signal estimate,
uniformly at every approximation level.

For $q\in\{2,6\}$ set
\[
 d_q^2=\frac1{10q^2(q-1)},\qquad
 \rho_q(z)=(1-z/d_q)^{-1/(10q)}.
\]
Choose $T_0$ so that all the fixed-$\tau$ limits satisfy
$0\le c^\tau\le\frac12\min\{d_2,d_6\}$ for $t\ge T_0$.
Then $1\le\rho_q(c^\tau)\le2^{1/(10q)}$.
We justify the joint test on the fixed-$\tau$ system, where the two scalar
transport velocities coincide.  This order is essential: applying it
directly before $\varepsilon\to0$ would leave a term containing
$(\mathcal J_\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon,\tau}
-u^{\varepsilon,\tau})\cdot\nabla c^{\varepsilon,\tau}$ multiplied by
a high power of $n^{\varepsilon,\tau}$, for which no uniform estimate
has been proved.

For $\lambda>0$ put
\[
 \Psi_{q,\lambda}(s)
   =q\int_0^s\frac{z^{q-1}}{1+\lambda z^{q-9/5}}\,dz.
\]
For fixed $q$ and $\lambda$, write $\Psi=\Psi_{q,\lambda}$ in the
following scalar identities. These functions increase to $s^q$ as
$\lambda\downarrow0$, have growth at infinity of order $s^{9/5}$,
and satisfy
\[
 \frac{(\Psi')^2}{\Psi\Psi''}\le\frac{5q}{4},\qquad
 \frac{s^2\Psi''}{\Psi}\le q(q-1).
\]
These are the elementary one-variable estimates of
Winkler~\cite[Lemma~6.1]{Winkler2017}.  Since
\[
 \frac{\rho_q'^2}{\rho_q\rho_q''}
    =\frac1{10q+1},\qquad
 \frac{\rho_q}{\rho_q''}
    =\frac{100q^2(d_q-z)^2}{10q+1},
\]
we have, for $s>0$ and $0\le z\le d_q/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-weight-structural}
 4(\Psi')^2(\rho_q')^2+s^2(\Psi'')^2\rho_q^2
 \le2\Psi\Psi''\rho_q\rho_q''.
\end{equation}
Indeed, after division by $\Psi\Psi''\rho_q\rho_q''$, the left side is
at most $15q/(10q+1)<2$.

For the joint-test calculation and its justification, up to the
removal of the spatial cutoffs below, fix $\tau>0$ and abbreviate
\[
 (n,c,u)=(n^\tau,c^\tau,u^\tau).
\]
In this part, the integrability bounds used to justify testing may
depend on $\tau$ and $\lambda$. The constants in the structural
inequality and the resulting smoothing bound do not.
The formal joint identity is

\begin{align*}
 \frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi(n)\rho_q(c)
 ={}&-\int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi''\rho_q|\nabla n|^2
     +\int_{\mathbb H^3}(a_\tau(n)\Psi''\rho_q-2\Psi'\rho_q')\nabla n\cdot\nabla c\\
    &-\int_{\mathbb H^3}(\Psi\rho_q''-a_\tau(n)\Psi'\rho_q')|\nabla c|^2
     -\int_{\mathbb H^3}h_\tau(n)c\Psi\rho_q'.
\end{align*}
Young's inequality, $0\le a_\tau(n)\le n$, and
\eqref{eq:eventual-weight-structural} leave one half of the first
dissipation on the left.  More explicitly, the coefficient produced
by absorbing the mixed term is bounded by
\[
 \frac{(a_\tau(n)\Psi''\rho_q-2\Psi'\rho_q')^2}{2\Psi''\rho_q}
       +a_\tau(n)\Psi'\rho_q'
 \le \frac{n^2\Psi''\rho_q}{2}
       +\frac{2(\Psi')^2(\rho_q')^2}{\Psi''\rho_q}
 \le\Psi\rho_q''.
\]

The resulting time-integrated inequality is legitimate under
\eqref{eq:eventual-fixed-tau-scalar-class}. One uses paired Steklov
time averages in the two scalar equations and then lets the averaging
length tend to zero, as in \cite[Lemma~5.1]{Winkler2017}. The required
products have the same integrability: $n,\nabla c\in L^4_{t,x}$,
$\nabla n\in L^2_{t,x}$, and $u\in L^{10/3}_{t,x}$ on finite positive
time intervals, so that $n h_\tau'(n)\nabla c\in L^2$,
$u\cdot\nabla n\in L^{5/4}$, and $u\cdot\nabla c\in L^{20/11}$.
For fixed $\lambda$, $\Psi''$ is bounded, and the test functions satisfy
$\Psi'(n)\in L^2\cap L^4\cap L^5$,
$a_\tau(n)\Psi''(n)\in L^4\cap L^5$, and
$\Psi(n)\in L^1\cap L^2\cap L^{20/9}$ on these space--time slabs.
In particular, $\nabla(\Psi'(n)\rho_q(c))\in L^2$, while the
concentration-test gradients converge in $L^{4/3}$ under time averaging.
For example, the terms $\Psi'(n)\nabla n\cdot\nabla c$,
$u\Psi'(n)\cdot\nabla n$, and $u\Psi(n)\cdot\nabla c$ are
integrable by H\"older's inequality with reciprocal exponents
$(1/4,1/2,1/4)$, $(3/10,1/5,1/2)$, and
$(3/10,9/20,1/4)$, respectively. Also,
$\Psi(n)|\nabla c|^2\in L^1$, and the reaction term is integrable
since $h_\tau(n)\le n$ and $n\in L^1\cap L^4$.
At zeros of the density, the tests are justified first by smooth
nonnegative regularization on bounded cylinders and then by
dominated convergence; no nonzero constant is integrated over $\mathbb H^3$.

The passage from bounded spatial cutoffs to $\mathbb H^3$ introduces no extra
term.  The common transport combines into
$\nabla\cdot(u\Psi(n)\rho_q(c))$; its flux belongs to $L^1$ on every
finite positive time interval because $u\in L^\infty_tL^2_x$,
$n\in L^\infty_t(L^1\cap L^4)$, and
$\Psi(n)\in L^\infty_tL^2_x$.  The other cutoff fluxes are integrable
for the same reason, using $\nabla n,\nabla c\in L^2_{t,x}$ and the
growth of $\Psi$ and $\Psi'$.  Their cutoff derivatives tend to zero.
The physical-boundary terms vanish by the weak Neumann conditions and
the zero velocity trace. We now restore the superscript $\tau$.
For the truncated weighted integral
\[
 F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}(t)
 :=\int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau(t))\rho_q(c^\tau(t)),
\]
the justified calculation gives
\[
 F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}(t)+\frac12\int_s^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}
       \Psi_{q,\lambda}''(n^\tau)\rho_q(c^\tau)|\nabla n^\tau|^2
 \le F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}(s).
\]
To obtain smoothing, we first estimate the truncated integral.
Its finite starting value will not need a uniform bound.

At almost every $s_\tau\in(T_0,T_0+1/4)$ the density belongs to
$L^1\cap L^2$, so that $F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}(s_\tau)<\infty$ for every fixed
$\lambda>0$.  Choose one such time for a countable sequence
$\lambda\downarrow0$ and both $q=2,6$.  The derivative-ratio estimate gives
\[
 \|\nabla\sqrt{\Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau)}\|_{L^2}^2
 \le\frac{5q}{16}\int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi_{q,\lambda}''(n^\tau)|\nabla n^\tau|^2,
 \qquad
 \int_{\mathbb H^3} \Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau)^{1/q}\le\int_{\mathbb H^3} n^\tau\le M.
\]
Only ordinary H\"older and Sobolev inequalities are needed; no
Banach-space property below exponent one is used. Indeed,
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau)
 &\le\left(\int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau)^{1/q}\right)^{2q/(3q-1)}
       \left(\int_{\mathbb H^3}\Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau)^3\right)^{(q-1)/(3q-1)}\\
 &\le C M^{2q/(3q-1)}
       \|\nabla\sqrt{\Psi_{q,\lambda}(n^\tau)}\|_{L^2}^{6(q-1)/(3q-1)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Consequently the truncated joint inequality gives
\[
 (F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau})'+c_q M^{-2q/[3(q-1)]}
       (F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau})^{1+2/[3(q-1)]}\le0.
\]
This holds in distributions, and hence for the nonincreasing
representative of $F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}$; downward jumps only strengthen the
comparison.  It follows that
\[
 F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}(t)\le C_q M^q(t-s_\tau)^{-3(q-1)/2},\qquad t>s_\tau,
\]
independently of $\lambda,\tau$ and of the size of
$F_{q,\lambda}^{\tau}(s_\tau)$.  Now monotone convergence removes $\lambda$.

For $t\ge T_0+1$, $t-s_\tau\ge\frac34(t-T_0)$, giving
\[
 \|n^\tau(t)\|_{L^q}
 \le C_qM(t-T_0)^{-\frac32(1-1/q)},\qquad q=2,6,
\]
with $C_q$ and $T_0$ independent of $\tau$. These bounds will pass to the final density $n$.

The weighted starting integrals are now finite at almost every
late time. Monotone convergence and Fatou's lemma therefore pass the
truncated energy inequality to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-weighted-density}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\int_{\mathbb H^3}(n^\tau(t))^q\rho_q(c^\tau(t))
 +\frac{2(q-1)}q\int_s^t\|\nabla(n^\tau)^{q/2}\|_{L^2}^2\\
 &\qquad\leq
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}(n^\tau(s))^q\rho_q(c^\tau(s)).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This also holds for almost every pair $T_0<s<t$ whenever its
right-hand side is finite. In particular, the $q=2$ inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eventual-density-tail}
 \int_t^\infty\|\nabla n^\tau\|_{L^2}^2
 \leq\int_{\mathbb H^3}(n^\tau(t))^2\rho_2(c^\tau(t))
 \le C M^2(t-T_0)^{-3/2}.
\end{equation}

Finally, let $\tau_j\downarrow0$ along the selection of
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection}, with $n^{\tau_j}\to n$ locally strongly.
Global Fatou lower semicontinuity and weak lower semicontinuity
of the gradient pass these $\tau$-uniform estimates to the final
limit, proving \eqref{eq:ev-density-decay} and
\eqref{eq:ev-density-tail} for $n$.

The tail estimate, first obtained for almost every starting time,
extends to every $t\geq T_0+1$ by continuity of the integral on the
left and of its upper bound. No convergence of a possibly unbounded
starting value is required.
The compactness, energy inequality, and suitability from
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection} are retained along this same diagonal
subsequence.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:ev-force-tail}
Assume additionally that $\phi\in L^\infty(\mathbb H^3)$, and let
$(n,c,u,p)$ be the solution selected in
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection}. For $R\geq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-gradient-L1-tail}
\int_{T_0+R}^{\infty}\|\nabla n(t)\|_{L^2}\,dt
\leq C M R^{-1/4}.
\end{equation}
In particular, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-effective-force}
g:=\mathbb P_+(\phi\nabla n),
\end{equation}
the velocity equation on $(T_0+1,\infty)$ can be written as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-projected-NS}
u_t+Au+\mathbb P_+(u\cdot\nabla u)=g,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-force-integrability}
g\in L^1(T_0+1,\infty;L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3))
\cap L^2(T_0+1,\infty;L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)).
\end{equation}
There is a good time $S>T_0+1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-velocity-finite-energy}
\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t\geq S}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+\int_S^\infty\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}^2\,dt<\infty.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:ev-density-tail} and Cauchy--Schwarz on the successive
intervals $(T_0+2^jR,T_0+2^{j+1}R)$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_{T_0+R}^{\infty}\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}
&\leq\sum_{j=0}^\infty (2^jR)^{1/2}
\left(\int_{T_0+2^jR}^\infty\|\nabla n\|_{L^2}^2\right)^{1/2}\\
&\leq C M R^{-1/4}\sum_{j=0}^\infty2^{-j/4}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:ev-gradient-L1-tail}. The identity
\[
-n\nabla\phi=-\nabla(n\phi)+\phi\nabla n
\]
holds in distributions. Since $n(t)\in H^1(\mathbb H^3)$ for almost every
late time and $\phi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb H^3)$, we have $n(t)\phi\in H^1(\mathbb H^3)$.
The Helmholtz projection therefore annihilates $\nabla(n\phi)$.
Its $L^2$ boundedness gives
\[
\|g(t)\|_{L^2}\leq\|\phi\|_{L^\infty}\|\nabla n(t)\|_{L^2}.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:ev-density-tail} this proves
\eqref{eq:ev-force-integrability}. The gradient $\nabla(n\phi)$ disappears only in the projected
equation; the pressure in the suitable quadruple remains $p$.

For almost every pair of admissible times the global kinetic
energy inequality from Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-kinetic-g}
\frac12\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^2+
\int_s^t\|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^2
\leq\frac12\|u(s)\|_{L^2}^2+\int_s^t(g,u)_{L^2}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $u\in H^1_0\cap L^2_\sigma$ implies
$-\int n\nabla\phi\cdot u=\int\phi\nabla n\cdot u$.
There is no boundary term, and a cutoff at infinity is justified
by $n,u\in L^2$ and $\phi\in W^{1,\infty}$.
Fix a good $S>T_0+1$. Since $g\in L^1_tL^2_x$, the energy
inequality on any finite interval yields
\[
\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{S<t<B}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}
\leq \|u(S)\|_{L^2}+2\int_S^\infty\|g(t)\|_{L^2}\,dt.
\]
This follows by solving the quadratic inequality for the essential
supremum of the $L^2$ norm.
Return to \eqref{eq:ev-kinetic-g} and let $B\to\infty$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:ev-velocity-finite-energy}.
This step uses the bounded potential, not a Poincar\'e inequality
on the unbounded domain.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:ev-velocity}
For the selected solution there is $t_*>S$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-velocity-strong}
\begin{aligned}
&u\in C([t_*,\infty);H^1_0(\mathbb H^3)\cap L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)),\\
\sup_{t\geq t_*}&\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+\int_{t_*}^\infty\|Au(t)\|_{L^2}^2\,dt<\infty.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In particular $u\in L^\infty(t_*,\infty;L^6(\mathbb H^3))$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Keep the force $g$ from \eqref{eq:ev-effective-force} fixed.
For a good restart time $s\geq S$ with
$u(s)\in H^1_0\cap L^2_\sigma$, let $v$ be the local strong solution of
\[
 v_t+Av+\mathbb P_+(v\cdot\nabla v)=g,\qquad v(s)=u(s).
\]
The energy argument in \eqref{eq:ev-kinetic-g} gives
\[
 \|v(t)\|_{L^2}
 \leq\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{r\geq S}\|u(r)\|_{L^2}
       +2\int_S^\infty\|g(r)\|_{L^2}\,dr\leq C
\]
throughout its existence interval, with a constant independent of
the restart time.
Local strong solvability and the $H^1$ continuation criterion
follow from the half-space Stokes estimates
\cite{DHP2001,DHP2003}; here $g\in L^2_{\rm loc}L^2$.

The homogeneous Sobolev and
Stokes elliptic estimates give
\[
\|v\|_{L^3}\leq C\|v\|_{L^2}^{1/2}
\|\nabla v\|_{L^2}^{1/2},
\qquad
\|\nabla v\|_{L^6}\leq C\|\nabla^2v\|_{L^2}
\leq C\|Av\|_{L^2}.
\]
Testing by $Av$ thus gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-enstrophy-bootstrap}
\begin{aligned}
\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla v\|_{L^2}^2
+\|Av\|_{L^2}^2
\leq C\|\nabla v\|_{L^2}^{1/2}\|Av\|_{L^2}^2+\frac14\|Av\|_{L^2}^2+\|g\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Choose $\eta>0$ so small that $C\eta^{1/4}\leq1/4$.
Since the two integrals in
\eqref{eq:ev-force-integrability} and
\eqref{eq:ev-velocity-finite-energy} have vanishing tails,
one can select a good time $t_*>S$ for the kinetic energy
inequality such that $u(t_*)\in H^1_0\cap L^2_\sigma$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-restart-smallness}
\|\nabla u(t_*)\|_{L^2}^2
+2\int_{t_*}^\infty\|g(t)\|_{L^2}^2\,dt\leq\frac\eta2.
\end{equation}

As long as $\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^2}^2\leq\eta$,
\eqref{eq:ev-enstrophy-bootstrap} implies
\[
\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^2}^2+\int_{t_*}^t\|Av\|_{L^2}^2
\leq\|\nabla v(t_*)\|_{L^2}^2
+2\int_{t_*}^t\|g\|_{L^2}^2\leq\frac\eta2.
\]
Continuity of $t\mapsto\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^2}^2$ excludes a first exit
from $\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^2}^2<\eta$.

The continuation criterion then yields a global strong solution
$v$ on $[t_*,\infty)$.

To identify $v$ with the selected weak velocity, test the difference of their equations.
The global energy inequality starting at the good time $t_*$,
the weak momentum identity and the strong equation for $v$
give the usual difference estimate, with the identical force
$g$ canceling. Its nonlinear term satisfies
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|\int_{\mathbb H^3}((u-v)\cdot\nabla)v\cdot(u-v)\right|
&=\left|\int_{\mathbb H^3}((u-v)\cdot\nabla)(u-v)\cdot v\right|\\
&\leq C\|v\|_{L^6}\|u-v\|_{L^2}^{1/2}
\|\nabla(u-v)\|_{L^2}^{3/2}\\
&\leq\frac12\|\nabla(u-v)\|_{L^2}^2
+C\|v\|_{L^6}^4\|u-v\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
These tests are justified by time regularization and approximation
of the strong solution in the solenoidal energy space.
Since $v\in L^\infty L^6$ on each finite interval, Gronwall's
inequality and $u(t_*)=v(t_*)$ imply $u=v$.
This use of weak--strong uniqueness is for a forced
Navier--Stokes equation with a fixed force and does not assume
weak--strong uniqueness of the coupled scalar--fluid system.
The preceding estimates prove \eqref{eq:ev-velocity-strong}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:ev-boundary-bootstrap}
There is $T_*>t_*$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-uniform-boundedness}
\sup_{t\geq T_*}
\bigl(\|n(t)\|_{L^\infty}
+\|c(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty}\bigr)<\infty.
\end{equation}
If, in addition,
$\nabla\phi\in C^{0,\beta_0}(\overline{\mathbb H^3})$ for some
$\beta_0\in(0,1)$, then after a further increase of $T_*$, for some
$\alpha\in(0,\beta_0)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-classical-tail}
n,c,u\in C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha/2}_{\rm loc}
\bigl(\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times(T_*,\infty)\bigr).
\end{equation}
The original equations and boundary conditions hold classically
there, with the corresponding local regularity of $\nabla p$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Start after $\max\{t_*,T_0+1\}$. The preceding lemmas give
$u,n\in L^\infty_tL^6_x$. We first obtain global bounds for $u$,
then for $\nabla c$, and finally for $n$. All estimates use sliding
time intervals of a fixed length, so their waiting times are
independent of the spatial center. Local boundary estimates then
give the asserted classical regularity.

\emph{Step 1: bounded velocity.}
For $1<a\leq b\leq\infty$, in the ranges used below, the
half-space Stokes estimates are
\begin{align*}
\|e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+F\|_{L^b}
&\leq C r^{-\frac32(\frac1a-\frac1b)}\|F\|_{L^a},\\
\|e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+\nabla\cdot\mathcal F\|_{L^b}
&\leq C r^{-\frac12-\frac32(\frac1a-\frac1b)}
\|\mathcal F\|_{L^a},\qquad 0<r\leq1;
\end{align*}
the second estimate is understood by the Stokes divergence
solution operator. Its $L^a\to L^a$ bound follows by duality from
the gradient estimate for the Stokes semigroup; a semigroup
factorization and the homogeneous Sobolev estimates give the displayed
$L^a\to L^b$ bounds, including the exponents used here
\cite{DHP2001}.
Use the mild formula with the original force $-n\nabla\phi$.
On a sliding interval of length one, the mapping from
$u\otimes u\in L^\infty_tL^3_x$ to $L^{12}$ has kernel
$r^{-7/8}$, and the mapping from $n\nabla\phi\in L^\infty_tL^6_x$
has kernel $r^{-1/8}$. Therefore, after a unit delay,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|u(s+1)\|_{L^{12}}
\leq C\bigl(&\|u\|_{L^\infty(s,s+1;L^6)}
+\|u\|_{L^\infty(s,s+1;L^6)}^2\\
&+\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}
\|n\|_{L^\infty(s,s+1;L^6)}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]
Apply the mild formula once more. Now $u\otimes u\in L^\infty L^6$,
and the two kernels for the $L^\infty$ estimate are $r^{-3/4}$
and $r^{-1/4}$. Both are integrable, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ev-u-infinity}
\begin{aligned}
\|u(s+2)\|_{L^\infty}
\leq C\bigl(&\|u\|_{L^\infty(s+1,s+2;L^{12})}
+\|u\|_{L^\infty(s+1,s+2;L^{12})}^2\\
&+\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}
\|n\|_{L^\infty(s+1,s+2;L^6)}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The two unit delays and the resulting bounds are uniform in $s$.

\emph{Step 2: bounded signal gradient.}
Use $\|c(t)\|_{L^\infty}\leq\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}$.
With $S_N$ the Neumann heat semigroup, the scalar mild formula on
$(s,s+h)$ gives
\[
c(s+r)=S_N(r)c(s)
-\int_0^r S_N(r-\sigma)
\bigl[u\cdot\nabla c+nc\bigr](s+\sigma)\,d\sigma.
\]
The gradient kernels for the two terms on the right are
$(r-\sigma)^{-1/2}$ on $L^\infty$ and
$(r-\sigma)^{-3/4}$ from $L^6$ to $L^\infty$.
Multiplying by $r^{1/2}$ and taking the supremum gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \sup_{0<r<h}r^{1/2}\|\nabla c(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}
 &\leq C\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}
 +Ch^{3/4}\|n\|_{L^\infty(s,s+h;L^6)}\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}\\
 &\quad+Ch^{1/2}\|u\|_{L^\infty(s,s+h;L^\infty)}
       \sup_{0<r<h}r^{1/2}\|\nabla c(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}.
\end{aligned}
\]
For completeness this estimate can first be applied to the
linear mild solution with bounded drift $u$ and given source
$-nc$. Its construction in the displayed weighted gradient
space follows by contraction for small $h$. The construction
is carried out simultaneously in
$C([s,s+h];L^2(\mathbb H^3))\cap
L^2(s,s+h;H^1(\mathbb H^3))$: here $c(s)\in L^2$ and
$nc\in L^\infty(s,s+h;L^2)$, and the $L^2$ energy estimate
is uniform along smooth approximations of the bounded drift.
Its equality with the weak concentration then follows from
the $L^2$ energy estimate for their difference. This justifies the
weighted gradient estimate without assuming it in advance for the
weak concentration. By the uniform velocity bound, choose
$h\in(0,1)$ so that the coefficient of the last supremum is at most
$1/2$ for every sufficiently late starting time $s$. Use sliding intervals of this
same length. This proves a uniform
$L^\infty$ bound for $\nabla c$ after another delay.

\emph{Step 3: bounded density.}
Write the density equation in flux form,
\[
n_t-\Delta n=-\nabla\cdot\bigl[n(u+\nabla c)\bigr].
\]
The flux has zero normal component, in the weak Neumann sense.
The Neumann divergence operator from
\eqref{eq:neumann-divergence-operator} maps $L^6$ to $L^\infty$
with kernel $r^{-3/4}$. Hence, on sliding intervals of the fixed length $h$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|n(s+h)\|_{L^\infty}
&\leq C h^{-1/4}\|n\|_{L^\infty(s,s+h;L^6)}\\
&\quad+C h^{1/4}\|n\|_{L^\infty(s,s+h;L^6)}
\sup_{s<t<s+h}\|u(t)+\nabla c(t)\|_{L^\infty}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The mild identity follows from the weak Neumann equation by
testing against the backward Neumann heat kernel; all fluxes
are in $L^\infty_tL^6_x$ on these intervals. This proves
\eqref{eq:ev-uniform-boundedness}.

\emph{Step 4: boundary H\"older regularity and classical equations.}
The concentration equation now has bounded right-hand side
$-u\cdot\nabla c-nc$. Local Neumann parabolic estimates give
$c\in W^{2,1}_r$ on each later cylinder meeting the boundary,
for every finite $r>1$. In particular, taking $r>5$ gives
H\"older continuity of $\nabla c$ on smaller closed cylinders.
These statements follow as well by even reflection of $c$.

For $n$, reflect the scalar evenly and the flux
$n(u+\nabla c)$ with even tangential and odd normal components.
The reflected equation has no measure supported on $\Gamma$,
by the weak no-flux identity. Its flux is bounded, and the heat
potential estimates imply local $C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}$ regularity
of $n$ for any fixed $\alpha\in(0,1)$ after shrinking cylinders.
For example, the spatial H\"older estimate for the gradient heat
potential uses the integrable kernel
$r^{-(1+\alpha)/2}$; the corresponding time-difference estimate
gives the exponent $\alpha/2$.

For the velocity, the strong estimates and boundedness of $u$ give
$u_t\in L^2$ on each finite strip, since $\nabla u,n\in L^2$ there.
Thus $u\in W^{2,1}_2$ and $\nabla u\in L^{10/3}$.
The source $-u\cdot\nabla u-n\nabla\phi$ consequently belongs to
$L^{10/3}$. After a temporal cutoff, Stokes maximal regularity gives
$u\in W^{2,1}_{10/3}$ on a smaller strip, hence $\nabla u\in L^{10}$.
Interpolation with the existing $L^2$ bound yields
\[
 \|\nabla u\|_{L^8}
 \le \|\nabla u\|_{L^2}^{1/16}
      \|\nabla u\|_{L^{10}}^{15/16}.
\]
A further Stokes estimate gives $u\in W^{2,1}_8$ and $\nabla p\in L^8$
\cite{DHP2001}. All norms here are on finite strips over the entire
half-space. The cutoff sources have the required integrability because
$u\in L^2\cap L^\infty$ and $n\in L^1\cap L^\infty$.
Parabolic embedding now gives H\"older continuity of $u$ and
$\nabla u$ up to $\Gamma$, with bounds independent of the spatial center.

Up to this point, only $\phi\in W^{1,\infty}$ has been used.
For the final Stokes Schauder step, now assume additionally that
$\nabla\phi\in C^{0,\beta_0}(\overline{\mathbb H^3})$.
Fix a compact time interval $I$ and a slightly larger interval $J$
in the bounded time tail.
Choose $\chi\in C_c^\infty(J)$ equal to one on $I$.
The pair $(\chi u,\chi p)$ solves the global half-space Stokes problem
with zero initial and boundary data and source
\[
 F=\chi(-u\cdot\nabla u-n\nabla\phi)+\chi'u
 \in L^\infty(J;L^2(\mathbb H^3))
       \cap C^{\beta,\beta/2}(\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times J)
\]
for some $\beta\in(0,\beta_0)$. Choose $\beta$ also smaller than
 the H\"older exponent already obtained for $n,u,\nabla u$. Then
\[
[n\nabla\phi]_{C^{\beta,\beta/2}}
\leq C\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}[n]_{C^{\beta,\beta/2}}
   +C\|n\|_{L^\infty}[\nabla\phi]_{C^{0,\beta}},
\]
where the norms are taken on the preceding space--time strip
(and spatially for $\nabla\phi$). Time independence of $\phi$
handles the time increments. This is the only step requiring
H\"older continuity of its gradient: boundedness of $\nabla\phi$
alone would not control the second term.
The $L^\infty_tL^2_x$ bound follows from boundedness of $u$ and the
uniform $L^2$ bounds for $u,\nabla u,n$.

The local Neumann elliptic Schauder estimate, combined with the global
$L^4$ Helmholtz estimate, gives, for $0<\alpha<1$,
\[
 \|\mathbb P_+F\|_{C^\alpha(\overline{\mathbb H^3})}
 +\|(I-\mathbb P_+)F\|_{C^\alpha(\overline{\mathbb H^3})}
 \le C_\alpha\bigl(\|F\|_{C^\alpha(\overline{\mathbb H^3})}
                       +\|F\|_{L^4}\bigr).
\]
Indeed, on unit half-balls the gradient of the Neumann potential is
controlled by the $C^\alpha$ norm of $F$ and its own local $L^4$ norm;
the latter is bounded by the global Helmholtz estimate. The same
argument applies on interior balls, with uniform constants.
For time differences, interpolation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \|F(t)-F(s)\|_{C^\alpha}
 &\le C|t-s|^{(\beta-\alpha)/2},\\
 \|F(t)-F(s)\|_{L^4}
 &\le \|F(t)-F(s)\|_{L^\infty}^{1/2}
       \|F(t)-F(s)\|_{L^2}^{1/2}
 \le C|t-s|^{\beta/4}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Hence both projected and gradient parts belong to
$C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}$ if $0<\alpha<\beta/2$.
The projected source is divergence free and has zero normal trace.

Apply the global half-space Stokes H\"older estimate
\cite[Theorem~1.1]{Solonnikov2003Half} to $\mathbb P_+F$.
To justify its use without a prescribed decay rate at infinity,
approximate $F$ by smooth spatially compactly supported functions,
still zero near the initial endpoint, uniformly bounded in
$L^\infty_tL^2_x\cap C^{\beta,\beta/2}$ and converging in $L^4_{t,x}$.
Project these approximations after truncation; they then have zero
normal trace and algebraic spatial decay. The preceding estimates
bound their H\"older norms uniformly. With zero initial and boundary
data, the cited Schauder estimate contains only the source H\"older
seminorms, with no decay constant. Maximal $L^4$ regularity controls
the lower-order norms and identifies the limit with $\chi u$.
Local compactness, with a smaller positive exponent if necessary,
passes the Schauder bounds to this same solution. Adding back the
gradient part of $F$ recovers the original pressure gradient. Thus
\[
 u\in C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha/2}_{\rm loc},
 \qquad \nabla p\in C^{\alpha,\alpha/2}_{\rm loc}
 \quad\text{on }\overline{\mathbb H^3}\times I.
\]
Using the global Stokes solution class here controls the
possible time-dependent harmonic pressure component.

The Neumann Schauder estimates applied to $c$ next give
$c\in C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha/2}_{\rm loc}$. Finally apply the
scalar boundary estimates to
\[
n_t-\Delta n+(u+\nabla c)\cdot\nabla n=-n\Delta c,
\qquad\partial_\nu n=0.
\]
Its coefficients and zeroth-order right-hand side have the
required H\"older regularity. This gives the same regularity
for $n$. These are local estimates on time intervals separated
from their initial endpoints; no compatibility at the original
time $t=0$ is needed. The preceding uniform global bounds ensure
that a single waiting time works for every interior and boundary
point. This proves \eqref{eq:ev-classical-tail}.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
When $M>0$, Lemmas~\ref{lem:ev-selection} and
\ref{lem:ev-scalar-decay} select a global suitable weak solution of
Theorem~\ref{thm:half-space-existence} with the global kinetic-energy
inequality and the scalar decay estimates. Lemmas~\ref{lem:ev-force-tail} and \ref{lem:ev-velocity}
give a global strong velocity after a finite time.
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-boundary-bootstrap} then proves the asserted
uniform bounds and, using the additional H\"older assumption in
\eqref{eq:eventual-potential-assumptions}, classical regularity up to
$\Gamma$. Increase the waiting time once more if necessary so that all
statements hold also at the left endpoint of the displayed
closed time interval.
If $M=0$, mass conservation and nonnegativity give $n=0$.
The selected velocity is then a Leray--Hopf solution of the
unforced half-space Navier--Stokes equations. The proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-velocity} applies with $g=0$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-boundary-bootstrap} applies to the remaining
linear concentration equation with $n=0$.
Finally, classical regularity implies regularity in the sense
of Definition~\ref{def:boundary-regular-point}; hence the full
singular set has no point at or after the resulting waiting time.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:asym-mass}
Let $(n,c,u,p)$ be the solution selected in
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection}.
For the weakly $L^1$-continuous representatives of the scalar
variables, and every $t\geq0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-mass-identities}
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}n(t)=M,\qquad
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)+\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}nc=L.
\end{equation}
In particular, $t\mapsto\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)$ is nonincreasing and locally absolutely
continuous, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-signal-mass-limit}
 L_\infty:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)
 =L-\int_0^\infty\int_{\mathbb H^3}nc\in[0,L].
\end{equation}
If $L>0$, then no finite-time loss of the entire signal mass
can occur: more precisely,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-finite-time-positive-mass}
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)\geq L\exp\left(-\frac{M\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}}{L}t\right)>0,
 \qquad 0\leq t<\infty.
\end{equation}
If $L=0$, then $c=0$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
\emph{Step 1: no mass is lost at spatial infinity in the
approximation limit.}
We first record this point for the concentration, in addition to
the density tightness established in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection}.  Let $\eta_R$ be a smooth radial
exterior cutoff, zero on $B_R$ and equal to one outside $B_{2R}$,
with $|D^j\eta_R|\leq CR^{-j}$ and
$\partial_\nu\eta_R=0$ on $\Gamma$.

Suppress the superscripts $(\varepsilon,\tau)$ in the next
cutoff estimate. Testing the regularized concentration equation and
dropping the nonnegative absorption term gives

\begin{align}
 \sup_{0<t<T}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)\eta_R
 &\leq\int_{\mathbb H^3}c_0^\varepsilon\eta_R
       +\frac{CLT}{R^2}
       +\frac C R\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3} c|u|\nonumber\\
 &\leq\int_{\mathbb H^3}c_0^\varepsilon\eta_R+\frac{C_T}{R}.
 \label{eq:asym-c-tightness}
\end{align}
Indeed, $\|c(t)\|_{L^{6/5}}\leq L^{5/6}\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}^{1/6}$ and
$u\in L^2(0,T;L^6(\mathbb H^3))$ uniformly in both parameters.  The initial
concentrations converge strongly in $L^1(\mathbb H^3)$, since
\[
 c_0^\varepsilon=(S_N(\varepsilon)\sqrt{c_0})^2,
 \qquad S_N(\varepsilon)\sqrt{c_0}\longrightarrow\sqrt{c_0}
             \quad\hbox{in }L^2(\mathbb H^3).
\]
Thus their $L^1$ tails are uniformly small, and
\eqref{eq:asym-c-tightness} is uniform spatial tightness on
every finite time interval.

Restoring the superscripts, local almost-everywhere convergence,
the uniform concentration bound, and spatial tightness give
\[
 c^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}\to c^\tau\quad(\tau\text{ fixed}),
 \qquad c^{\tau_j}\to c\quad(\tau_j\downarrow0)
 \quad\text{in }L^1(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)).
\]
  The corresponding strong convergences
$n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}\to n^\tau$ and $n^{\tau_j}\to n$ in
$L^1(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T))$ follow from the density tightness and
uniform integrability proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-selection}; see also
\eqref{eq:eventual-global-force-compactness}.

The absorption terms converge strongly on the whole finite
space--time slab.  At fixed $\tau$, the $1$-Lipschitz property of $h_\tau$,
the global strong $L^1$ convergence $n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}\to n^\tau$,
and bounded almost-everywhere convergence
$c^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}\to c^\tau$ give

\[
 h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon_j,\tau})c^{\varepsilon_j,\tau}
 \longrightarrow h_\tau(n^\tau)c^\tau
 \quad\text{in }L^1(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T)).
\]
For the product with $h_\tau(n^\tau)$, use dominated convergence
and $h_\tau(n^\tau)\le n^\tau$.
At the subsequent limit $\tau_j\to0$, the same conclusion follows from
\[
 \|h_{\tau_j}(n^{\tau_j})-n\|_{L^1_{t,x}}
 \leq\|n^{\tau_j}-n\|_{L^1_{t,x}}
       +\|h_{\tau_j}(n)-n\|_{L^1_{t,x}}\longrightarrow0,
\]
and
\[
 \|h_{\tau_j}(n^{\tau_j})c^{\tau_j}-nc\|_{L^1_{t,x}}
 \leq \|c_0\|_{L^\infty}\|h_{\tau_j}(n^{\tau_j})-n\|_{L^1_{t,x}}
       +\int_0^T\int_{\mathbb H^3}n|c^{\tau_j}-c|\longrightarrow0.
\]
Here $0\leq h_\tau(n)\leq n$ and $n\in L^1(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T))$ justify
both dominated-convergence arguments.  Hence there is no defect
measure in the total absorption.

On the smooth approximation,
\[
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}n^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)=M,\qquad
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)
 +\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}h_\tau(n^{\varepsilon,\tau})c^{\varepsilon,\tau}
 =\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}\longrightarrow L.
\]
These identities pass to both limits for almost every time.
To obtain representatives at every time, observe that the weak
scalar equations give locally absolutely continuous pairings with
smooth compactly supported spatial tests. The entropy bound for
$n$, boundedness of $c$, and the uniform spatial tails give weak
$L^1$ compactness on each finite time interval. Thus any sequence
of admissible times approaching a fixed time has a weak $L^1$
cluster point, uniquely determined by those continuous pairings.

The convergence of the pairings extends to bounded measurable
tests. First discard the uniformly small spatial tails; on the
remaining bounded set, Lusin approximation and uniform integrability
control the error in replacing such a test by a continuous one.
Smooth approximation then applies. This gives weakly
$L^1$-continuous representatives, including at $t=0$, for which
\eqref{eq:asym-mass-identities} holds at every time. On the classical
time interval these are the classical representatives. The signal
identity gives local absolute continuity of its total mass, and
monotone convergence gives \eqref{eq:asym-signal-mass-limit}.

\emph{Step 2: positivity of the signal mass at finite times.}
Assume $L>0$. Fix the approximation parameters and, within this
step, suppress the superscripts on $(n,c,u)$. Let $q$ solve the
passive scalar problem
\[
 q_t+u\cdot\nabla q=\Delta q,\qquad
 \partial_\nu q=0,\qquad q(0)=c_0^\varepsilon.
\]
Incompressibility, the boundary conditions, and the maximum
principle give
\[
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}q(t)=\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}>0,\qquad
 0<q(t)\leq\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}.
\]
The initial regularization is strictly positive by positivity of
the Neumann kernel. Comparison with the concentration equation
therefore gives
\[
 e^{-\int_0^t\|h_\tau(n(s))\|_{L^\infty}\,ds}q(t)
 \leq c(t)\leq q(t).
\]
In particular, the relative logarithmic density is well defined and
\[
 0\leq q(t)\log\frac{q(t)}{c(t)}
 \leq q(t)\int_0^t\|h_\tau(n(s))\|_{L^\infty}\,ds.
\]
Using the common transport velocity, direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-relative-logarithmic-identity}
 \begin{aligned}
 (\partial_t+u\cdot\nabla-\Delta)\left(q\log\frac qc\right)
 &=-q\left|\nabla\log\frac qc\right|^2+h_\tau(n)q,\\
 \partial_\nu\left(q\log\frac qc\right)&=0.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This identity can be integrated over the half-space. Indeed,
insert the cutoff $1-\eta_R$ from Step~1. On any fixed interval
$(0,T)$, the diffusion and transport errors are bounded by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 C\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}
 \left(R^{-2}+R^{-1}\|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3\times(0,T))}\right)
 \int_0^T\int_0^t\|h_\tau(n(s))\|_{L^\infty}\,ds\,dt
 \longrightarrow0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The coefficients are bounded for fixed approximation parameters.
Fatou's lemma handles the nonnegative gradient term, and the
initial logarithmic density is zero. Since $h_\tau(n)\leq n$,
we obtain the parameter-independent estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-relative-entropy-bound}
 \int_{\mathbb H^3}q(t)\log\frac{q(t)}{c(t)}
 \leq\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb H^3}h_\tau(n)q
 \leq M\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}t.
\end{equation}
Jensen's inequality for the probability measure
$q(t,x)\,dx/\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}$ gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 -\frac1{\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}}
       \int_{\mathbb H^3}q(t)\log\frac{q(t)}{c(t)}
 &\leq
 \log\left(\frac{\|c(t)\|_{L^1}}{\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}}\right).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Restoring the superscripts, it follows that
\[
 \|c^{\varepsilon,\tau}(t)\|_{L^1}
 \geq\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}
 \exp\left(-\frac{M\|c_0\|_{L^\infty}}
                  {\|c_0^\varepsilon\|_{L^1}}\,t\right).
\]
The global strong $L^1$ convergence from Step~1 passes this bound
to the selected solution for almost every time, first as
$\varepsilon\to0$ and then as $\tau\to0$. Continuity of the total
signal mass extends it to every finite time, proving
\eqref{eq:asym-finite-time-positive-mass}. If $L=0$, nonnegativity
and \eqref{eq:asym-mass-identities} give $c=0$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:asym-velocity}
For the global solution selected in Section~\ref{sec:eventual-regularity},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-L2-H1}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}=0,
 \qquad
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^{1/2}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}=0.
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every $2<p\le\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-Lp}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}
 t^{\frac32(\frac12-\frac1p)}\|u(t)\|_{L^p}=0,
\end{equation}
where $1/\infty=0$.  In particular, $u(t)\to0$ strongly in
$H^1_0(\mathbb H^3)$ and uniformly on $\overline{\mathbb H^3}$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Choose $S\ge1$ sufficiently large that the strong-solution and
boundedness conclusions of Section~\ref{sec:eventual-regularity}
hold on $[S,\infty)$.  Recall that
$g=\mathbb P_+(\phi\nabla n)$.
By \eqref{eq:ev-force-integrability}, \eqref{eq:ev-density-tail},
and Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-velocity},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-tail-inputs}
\begin{gathered}
 \sup_{t\ge S}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}<\infty,\qquad
 \int_S^\infty\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}^2\,dt<\infty,\qquad
 g\in L^1((S,\infty);L^2_\sigma(\mathbb H^3)),\\
 \int_t^\infty\|g(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds
 \le \|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^2
       \int_t^\infty\|\nabla n(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds
 \le C(1+t)^{-3/2}\qquad(t\ge S).
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The last estimate is understood with a larger constant if necessary
to replace $t-T_0$ by $1+t$.  The velocity energy relation and the
closed bootstrap in \eqref{eq:ev-enstrophy-bootstrap} give, for
almost every $t\ge S$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-differential-energies}
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 +2\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 &\le 2(g(t),u(t))_{L^2},\\
 \frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 +\|Au(t)\|_{L^2}^2&\le2\|g(t)\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
All time-dependent estimates below use the continuous strong-solution
representative of Lemma~\ref{lem:ev-velocity}.

\emph{Step 1: decay of the kinetic energy.}
The eventual $H^1$ bound and the finite total dissipation imply
\[
 \int_S^\infty\|u(s)\|_{L^4}^4\,ds
 \le C\sup_{s\ge S}\|u(s)\|_{L^2}
       \sup_{s\ge S}\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^2}
       \int_S^\infty\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds<\infty.
\]
Fix $T\ge S$ and put $z(t)=u(t)-e^{-(t-T)A}u(T)$.
Then $z(T)=0$ and
$z_t+Az=-\mathbb P_+\operatorname{div}(u\otimes u)+g$.
Testing by $z$ is justified by the eventual strong regularity.
Young's inequality for the convection term gives, for every finite $B>T$,
\[
 \sup_{T\le t\le B}\|z(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 \le \int_T^B\|u(s)\|_{L^4}^4\,ds
   +2\left(\sup_{T\le t\le B}\|z(t)\|_{L^2}\right)
       \int_T^B\|g(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds.
\]
Solving this quadratic inequality and then letting $B\to\infty$ yields
\[
 \sup_{t\ge T}\|z(t)\|_{L^2}
 \le \left(\int_T^\infty\|u(s)\|_{L^4}^4\,ds\right)^{1/2}
       +2\int_T^\infty\|g(s)\|_{L^2}\,ds.
\]
The nonnegative self-adjoint Dirichlet Stokes operator has trivial
kernel: $Av=0$ implies $\|\nabla v\|_{L^2}=0$, and hence $v=0$ in
$L^2(\mathbb H^3)$. The spectral theorem therefore gives
$\|e^{-(t-T)A}u(T)\|_{L^2}\to0$ for fixed $T$.
Thus $\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|u(t)\|_{L^2}$ is bounded by the last
display's right-hand side. Letting $T\to\infty$, using the first
display and $g\in L^1((S,\infty);L^2)$, proves the claim.

\emph{Step 2: decay of the velocity gradient.}
Integrating the second inequality in
\eqref{eq:asym-velocity-differential-energies} from $s$ to $t$ and
then averaging over $s\in[t/2,t]$, for $t\ge2S$, yields
\begin{align}
 \|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}^2
 &\le\frac2t\int_{t/2}^t\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds
       +2\int_{t/2}^\infty\|g(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds\nonumber\\
 &=o(t^{-1})+O(t^{-3/2}).
 \label{eq:asym-gradient-rate}
\end{align}
Here the first term is $o(t^{-1})$ because
\[
\int_S^\infty\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds<\infty.
\]
Consequently $t^{1/2}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^2}\to0$, and the homogeneous
Sobolev inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-L6-preliminary}
 \|u(t)\|_{L^6}=o(t^{-1/2}).
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 3: a preliminary uniform decay estimate.}
We will use the half-space Stokes estimates, for the exponents
appearing below,
\begin{align}
 \|e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+F\|_{L^b}
 &\le C r^{-\frac32(\frac1a-\frac1b)}\|F\|_{L^a},
 \nonumber\\
 \|e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+\operatorname{div}\mathcal F\|_{L^b}
 &\le C r^{-\frac12-\frac32(\frac1a-\frac1b)}
             \|\mathcal F\|_{L^a},
 \qquad r>0.
 \label{eq:asym-stokes-smoothing}
\end{align}
Here the input exponents used below satisfy $2\le a<\infty$,
and $a\le b\le\infty$. We give the endpoint justification.
Use the same notation $A$ for the consistent Stokes operators on
$L^a_\sigma$. For $1<a<\infty$, analyticity and the homogeneous
elliptic estimate give \cite{DHP2001}
\[
\|e^{-rA}v\|_{L^a}+r\|\nabla^2e^{-rA}v\|_{L^a}\le C_a\|v\|_{L^a},
\qquad
\|\nabla e^{-rA}v\|_{L^a}\le C_a r^{-1/2}\|v\|_{L^a}.
\]
Duality with the gradient estimate at the conjugate exponent
defines the divergence operator and yields
\[
\|e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+\operatorname{div}\mathcal F\|_{L^a}
\le C_a r^{-1/2}\|\mathcal F\|_{L^a}.
\]
For $a\ge2$, the homogeneous Gagliardo--Nirenberg inequality
\[
\|w\|_{L^b}
\le C\|\nabla^2w\|_{L^a}^{\frac32(\frac1a-\frac1b)}
       \|w\|_{L^a}^{1-\frac32(\frac1a-\frac1b)},
\]
has derivative exponent less than one, includes $b=\infty$,
and gives the first estimate in
\eqref{eq:asym-stokes-smoothing}. Factoring the semigroup at
$r/2$ and applying the preceding same-exponent divergence bound
gives the second one. Consistency on intersections identifies
these operators for all exponents used. The constants are valid
for every $r>0$: the dilation $x=r^{1/2}y$ preserves the half-space
and the no-slip Stokes problem. No $L^1$ or $L^\infty$ input
endpoint is used.

Equation~\eqref{eq:asym-velocity-L6-preliminary} and
\eqref{eq:ev-density-decay} imply
\[
 \|u\|_{L^\infty(t,\infty;L^6)}=o(t^{-1/2}),
 \qquad \|n\|_{L^\infty(t,\infty;L^6)}=O(t^{-5/4}).
\]
The first bound follows directly from the definition of the
pointwise small-$o$ estimate. Apply the mild formula on sliding
intervals of length one,
using the original force $-n\nabla\phi$. The $L^3\to L^{12}$
divergence kernel is $r^{-7/8}$, and the $L^6\to L^{12}$ force
kernel is $r^{-1/8}$. Therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \|u\|_{L^\infty(t+1,\infty;L^{12})}
 \le C\bigl(\|u\|_{L^\infty(t,\infty;L^6)}
             +\|u\|_{L^\infty(t,\infty;L^6)}^2
+\|n\|_{L^\infty(t,\infty;L^6)}\bigr)
 =o(t^{-1/2}).
\end{aligned}
\]
The constants here absorb $\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^\infty}$.
On another unit interval the nonlinear tensor belongs to $L^6$,
and the relevant kernels are $r^{-3/4}$ and $r^{-1/4}$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-infinity-preliminary}
\begin{aligned}
 \|u\|_{L^\infty(t+2,\infty;L^\infty)}
 &\le C\bigl(\|u\|_{L^\infty(t+1,\infty;L^{12})}
             +\|u\|_{L^\infty(t+1,\infty;L^{12})}^2\\
 &\qquad\quad+\|n\|_{L^\infty(t+1,\infty;L^6)}\bigr)
 =o(t^{-1/2}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus the preliminary decay is uniform on all sufficiently late
sliding intervals.

\emph{Step 4: the $L^\infty$ decay rate.}
Use the mild formula on the longer interval $[t/2,t]$, where
$t\ge\max\{2S,4\}$, and write $r=t-s$:
\begin{align}
 u(t)
 ={}&e^{-(t/2)A}u(t/2)
 -\int_0^{t/2}e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+
       \operatorname{div}(u\otimes u)(t-r)\,dr
 +\int_0^{t/2}e^{-rA}g(t-r)\,dr.
 \label{eq:asym-long-window-mild}
\end{align}
By Step~1 and \eqref{eq:asym-stokes-smoothing}, the first term satisfies
\[
 \|e^{-(t/2)A}u(t/2)\|_{L^{\infty}}
 \le Ct^{-3/4}\|u(t/2)\|_{L^2}=o(t^{-3/4}).
\]
For the nonlinear term split its integral at $r=1$.  On $(0,1)$,
use
$\|u\otimes u\|_{L^6}\le\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u\|_{L^6}$ and the
$L^6\to L^\infty$ divergence estimate.  On $(1,t/2)$, use
$\|u\otimes u\|_{L^3}\le\|u\|_{L^6}^2$ and the
$L^3\to L^\infty$ estimate.  This gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-long-window-nonlinearity}
\begin{aligned}
 &\left\|\int_0^{t/2}e^{-rA}\mathbb P_+
       \operatorname{div}(u\otimes u)(t-r)\,dr\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
 &\quad\le C\|u\|_{L^\infty(t/2,\infty;L^\infty)}
                   \|u\|_{L^\infty(t/2,\infty;L^6)}
          \int_0^1r^{-3/4}\,dr\\
 &\qquad+C\|u\|_{L^\infty(t/2,\infty;L^6)}^2
          \int_1^{t/2}r^{-1}\,dr\\
 &\quad=o(t^{-1})+o(t^{-1}\log t)=o(t^{-3/4}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

For the force, two representations are useful.  At almost every
late time, $n\phi\in H^1(\mathbb H^3)$ and the Helmholtz projection annihilates
$\nabla(n\phi)$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-force-two-representations}
 g=\mathbb P_+(\phi\nabla n)
   =-\mathbb P_+(n\nabla\phi).
\end{equation}
The Helmholtz projections and Stokes semigroups in $L^2$ and
$L^6$ are consistent on their intersection.

Since $\sqrt t\le t/2$, on $0<r<\sqrt t$ use the second
representation, \eqref{eq:ev-density-decay}, and the
$L^6\to L^\infty$ estimate to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-recent-force}
\begin{aligned}
 \left\|\int_0^{\sqrt t}e^{-rA}g(t-r)\,dr\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
 &\le C\|n\|_{L^\infty(t/2,\infty;L^6)}
          \int_0^{\sqrt t}r^{-1/4}\,dr\\
 &\le Ct^{-5/4}t^{3/8}=Ct^{-7/8}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
On $\sqrt t<r<t/2$, use the $L^2\to L^\infty$ estimate,
Cauchy--Schwarz in time, and \eqref{eq:asym-velocity-tail-inputs}:
\begin{align}
 \left\|\int_{\sqrt t}^{t/2}e^{-rA}g(t-r)\,dr\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
 &\le C\left(\int_{\sqrt t}^\infty r^{-3/2}\,dr\right)^{1/2}
         \left(\int_{t/2}^\infty\|g(s)\|_{L^2}^2\,ds\right)^{1/2}
 \nonumber\\
 &\le C t^{-1/8}t^{-3/4}=Ct^{-7/8}.
 \label{eq:asym-earlier-force}
\end{align}

Thus the free evolution is $o(t^{-3/4})$, the convection term
has the same bound, and both force contributions are $O(t^{-7/8})$.
Substituting these estimates in \eqref{eq:asym-long-window-mild}
proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-velocity-infinity}
 \|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=o(t^{-3/4}).
\end{equation}
Finally, for $2<p<\infty$,
\[
 \|u(t)\|_{L^p}
 \le\|u(t)\|_{L^2}^{2/p}\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1-2/p},
\]
which, together with Step~1 and
\eqref{eq:asym-velocity-infinity}, yields
\eqref{eq:asym-velocity-Lp}.  The $H^1$ convergence follows from
\eqref{eq:asym-velocity-L2-H1}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:asym-scalars}
For all sufficiently large $t$ and every $1\leq p\leq\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-scalar-rates}
 \|n(t)\|_{L^p}+\|c(t)\|_{L^p}\leq C_p t^{-\alpha_p}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-signal-gradient}
 \|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
All intervals in this proof lie inside the classical time interval
provided by Theorem~\ref{thm:eventual-regularity}. The scalar bounds
initially valid almost everywhere in time extend to every such time
by local continuity and Fatou's lemma.

For large $s$, Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-velocity} and the previously established scalar
estimates imply
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \|u\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}=o(s^{-1/2}),\qquad
 \|nc\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}=O(s^{-11/4}),\\
 \|c(s)\|_{L^{\infty}}=O(s^{-3/2}).
\end{gathered}
\]
The concentration mild formula and \eqref{eq:asym-neumann-bounds} give,
for $0<r\leq s$,
\begin{align*}
 \|\nabla c(s+r)\|_{L^{\infty}}
 &\leq C r^{-1/2}\|c(s)\|_{L^{\infty}}
   +C \|u\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}\int_0^r(r-\sigma)^{-3/4}
                    \|\nabla c(s+\sigma)\|_{L^{\infty}}\,d\sigma\\
 &\qquad+C \|nc\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}r^{1/4}.
\end{align*}
Multiply by $r^{1/2}$ and take the supremum over $0<r\leq s$.
The time-kernel estimate
\[
 r^{1/2}\int_0^r(r-\sigma)^{-3/4}\sigma^{-1/2}\,d\sigma
 \leq Cr^{1/4}
\]
gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sup_{0<r\leq s}r^{1/2}\|\nabla c(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}
 &\leq C\|c(s)\|_{L^\infty}
       +Cs^{3/4}\|nc\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}\\
 &\quad+Cs^{1/4}\|u\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}
       \sup_{0<r\leq s}r^{1/2}\|\nabla c(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The supremum is finite by the eventual gradient bound already
proved. The coefficient of the last term tends to zero, while
$s^{3/4}\|nc\|_{L^\infty(s,2s;L^6)}=O(s^{-2})$.
Absorption therefore gives
\[
 \sup_{0<r\leq s}r^{1/2}\|\nabla c(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}
 \leq Cs^{-3/2}.
\]
Taking $r=s$ proves \eqref{eq:asym-signal-gradient}.

For the density equation, the combined transport satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-small-rescaled-drift}
 \sqrt s\bigl(
 \|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3\times[s,2s])}
 +\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3\times[s,2s])}
 \bigr)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Its mild formula is
\[
 n(s+r)=S_N(r)n(s)
 -\int_0^r\mathcal D_N(r-\sigma)
       [n(u+\nabla c)](s+\sigma)\,d\sigma.
\]
The flux has zero normal component, so this is the mild form of the
classical no-flux equation. The $L^2\to L^\infty$ heat estimate,
the $L^\infty\to L^\infty$ divergence estimate, and
\[
 r^{3/4}\int_0^r(r-\sigma)^{-1/2}\sigma^{-3/4}\,d\sigma
 \leq Cr^{1/2}
\]
yield
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sup_{0<r\leq s}r^{3/4}\|n(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}
 &\leq C\|n(s)\|_{L^2}\\
 &\quad+C\sqrt s\bigl(
       \|u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3\times[s,2s])}
       +\|\nabla c\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb H^3\times[s,2s])}\bigr)\\
 &\qquad\qquad\times
       \sup_{0<r\leq s}r^{3/4}\|n(s+r)\|_{L^\infty}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Again the supremum is finite by eventual boundedness. Absorbing
the last term using \eqref{eq:asym-small-rescaled-drift} and taking
$r=s$ gives
\[
 \|n(2s)\|_{L^\infty}
 \leq Cs^{-3/4}\|n(s)\|_{L^2}\leq Cs^{-3/2},
\]
where \eqref{eq:ev-density-decay} was used in the last step.
The calculation can first be made at admissible starting times;
continuity on the classical interval and Fatou's lemma extend
the bound to every sufficiently large terminal time.
Interpolation with $\|n(t)\|_{L^1}=M$ proves the density bounds in
\eqref{eq:asym-scalar-rates}. For $c$, interpolate its mass bound
with \eqref{eq:ev-signal-decay}.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:asym-residual-mass}
The remaining mass in \eqref{eq:asym-signal-mass-limit} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-residual-mass}
 0<L_\infty\leq L\qquad\text{if }L>0.
\end{equation}
If $L=0$, then $c\equiv0$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
If $L>0$, choose any sufficiently large time $T$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-mass} gives $\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(T)>0$.
The mass identity and Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-scalars} give
\[
 \frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t)
 =-\int_{\mathbb H^3}nc
 \geq-\|n(t)\|_{L^\infty}\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(t),
 \qquad
 \int_T^\infty\|n(t)\|_{L^\infty}\,dt<\infty.
\]
Integrating this differential inequality yields
\[
 L_\infty\geq
 \left(\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(T)\right)
 \exp\left(-\int_T^\infty\|n(t)\|_{L^\infty}\,dt\right)>0.
\]
For $L=0$, nonnegativity and the mass inequality force $c=0$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:asym-heat-profile}
Let $G_N$ be the unit-mass Neumann Gaussian defined in
Theorem~\ref{thm:large-time-asymptotics}. For $f\in L^1(\mathbb H^3)$, a fixed $T\geq0$, and
$1\leq p\leq\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-linear-profile}
  t^{\alpha_p}
 \left\|S_N(t-T)f-\left(\int_{\mathbb H^3}f\right)G_N(t)\right\|_{L^p}
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Suppose a vector field $F$ belongs to
$L^1((T,B);L^1(\mathbb H^3))$ for every $B>T$ and satisfies, for this $p$,
\[
 \|F(s)\|_{L^1}=o(s^{-1/2}),\qquad
 \|F(s)\|_{L^p}=o(s^{-\alpha_p-1/2}).
\]
Then, whenever the following integral is defined (as it is for the
classical fluxes below),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-flux-remainder}
 t^{\alpha_p}
 \left\|\int_T^t\mathcal D_N(t-s)F(s)\,ds\right\|_{L^p}
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Extend $f$ evenly to $f^e$ on $\mathbb R^3$. Its integral is $2\int_{\mathbb H^3}f$,
and $S_N(r)f=(G(\cdot,r)*f^e)|_{\mathbb H^3}$, where $G$ is the whole-space
Gaussian. For every fixed $y\in\mathbb R^3$,
\[
 r^{\alpha_p}\|G(\cdot-y,r)-G(\cdot,r)\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^3)}
 =\|G(\cdot-y/\sqrt r,1)-G(\cdot,1)\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^3)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
This follows from translation continuity for finite $p$, and from
uniform continuity of the Gaussian for $p=\infty$.
The expression is bounded by $2\|G(\cdot,1)\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^3)}$.
Minkowski's inequality and dominated convergence against $|f^e(y)|$
prove the assertion with $r=t-T$ and $G_N(t-T)$.
Replacing $t-T$ by $t$ in the Gaussian changes the normalized norm
by a quantity tending to zero, by dilation continuity of the Gaussian
and $(t-T)/t\to1$. This proves \eqref{eq:asym-linear-profile}
without any spatial moment assumption.

For the second assertion, split the time integral at $t/2$.
The early part satisfies
\[
 \left\|\int_T^{t/2}\mathcal D_N(t-s)F(s)\,ds\right\|_{L^p}
 \leq C t^{-\alpha_p-1/2}\int_T^{t/2}\|F(s)\|_{L^1}\,ds
 =o(t^{-\alpha_p}).
\]
The last equality follows by separating a fixed initial interval and
using $\|F(s)\|_{L^1}=o(s^{-1/2})$ on the rest.
For the late part, use the $L^p\to L^p$ divergence bound:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \left\|\int_{t/2}^t\mathcal D_N(t-s)F(s)\,ds\right\|_{L^p}
 &\leq C\left(\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t/2<s<t}\|F(s)\|_{L^p}\right)
       \int_{t/2}^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\,ds\\
 &=o(t^{-\alpha_p-1/2})\,O(t^{1/2})
 =o(t^{-\alpha_p}).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Both estimates use \eqref{eq:asym-neumann-bounds}, so they include
$p=1$ and $p=\infty$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:asym-gaussian}
For every $1\leq p\leq\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-gaussian-profiles}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^{\alpha_p}
 \left(\|n(t)-MG_N(t)\|_{L^p}
       +\|c(t)-L_\infty G_N(t)\|_{L^p}\right)=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Fix $T$ after the waiting times of the preceding lemmas.
For every $1\leq p\leq\infty$, the density flux satisfies
\[
 \|n(t)(u(t)+\nabla c(t))\|_{L^p}
 \leq\|n(t)\|_{L^p}
       \bigl(\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty}+\|\nabla c(t)\|_{L^\infty}\bigr)
 =o(t^{-\alpha_p-1/2}).
\]
For $p=1$ this is $o(t^{-1/2})$, and the flux is integrable on
every finite interval starting at $T$. Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-heat-profile} to
\[
 n(t)=S_N(t-T)n(T)
       -\int_T^t\mathcal D_N(t-s)[n(u+\nabla c)](s)\,ds.
\]
Since $\int_{\mathbb H^3}n(T)=M$, this gives the density profile.

The concentration mild formula is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-c-mild-profile}
 \begin{aligned}
 c(t)={}&S_N(t-T)c(T)
 -\int_T^t\mathcal D_N(t-s)(uc)(s)\,ds\\
 &-\int_T^tS_N(t-s)(nc)(s)\,ds.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here incompressibility and the zero boundary trace of $u$ justify
the divergence form of the transport term. The flux $uc$ satisfies
the same small-$o$ bounds as the density flux, so its contribution
is $o(t^{-\alpha_p})$. The absorption term satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-reaction-decay}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|(nc)(s)\|_{L^p}
 &\leq\|n(s)\|_{L^\infty}\|c(s)\|_{L^p}
 \leq C_p s^{-\alpha_p-3/2},\\
 nc&\in L^1((T,\infty)\times\mathbb H^3).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
By the mass identity, its total integral from $T$ onward equals
$\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(T)-L_\infty$. We claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asym-reaction-profile}
 t^{\alpha_p}
 \left\|\int_T^tS_N(t-s)(nc)(s)\,ds
 -\left(\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(T)-L_\infty\right)G_N(t)\right\|_{L^p}
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}

For almost every fixed $s$, the linear profile estimate gives
\[
 t^{\alpha_p}
 \left\|S_N(t-s)(nc)(s)
       -\left(\int_{\mathbb H^3}(nc)(s)\right)G_N(t)\right\|_{L^p}
 \longrightarrow0.
\]
On $T<s<t/2$, the expression is bounded by
$C_p\|(nc)(s)\|_{L^1}$, which is integrable in $s$.
Dominated convergence handles this early part. The omitted mass
$\int_{t/2}^\infty\int_{\mathbb H^3}nc$ tends to zero, and
$t^{\alpha_p}\|G_N(t)\|_{L^p}$ is constant. For the late part,
contractivity and \eqref{eq:asym-reaction-decay} give
\[
 \left\|\int_{t/2}^tS_N(t-s)(nc)(s)\,ds\right\|_{L^p}
 \leq C_p\int_{t/2}^t s^{-\alpha_p-3/2}\,ds
 \leq C_p t^{-\alpha_p-1/2}.
\]
These estimates include $p=1$ and $p=\infty$, and prove
\eqref{eq:asym-reaction-profile}.

Finally, the linear term in \eqref{eq:asym-c-mild-profile}
contributes $\bigl(\int_{\mathbb H^3}c(T)\bigr)G_N(t)$.
Subtracting the absorption profile leaves exactly
$L_\infty G_N(t)$. This proves \eqref{eq:asym-gaussian-profiles}.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Use the same selected solution as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:eventual-regularity}; no further choice or change of
initial data is involved. Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-mass} gives the global
mass identities. Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-velocity} gives the velocity
limits and rates, Lemma~\ref{lem:asym-scalars} gives the scalar rates,
and Lemmas~\ref{lem:asym-residual-mass} and \ref{lem:asym-gaussian}
give the remaining signal mass and both Gaussian profiles.
If $M=0$, then $n=0$ and $g=0$; the velocity argument remains valid
for the unforced equation and the scalar proofs apply with the density
terms omitted. If $L=0$, then $c=0$ and its profile is identically
zero. Thus the conclusions also include both degenerate cases.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
